DRESS-FASHION-HOME. PARASOLS FOR THE SEASIDE.

The seaside parasol although not a novelty it of the utmost importance, declares a Taticri writer, and when not of the same colour as the costume should be of that peculiar shade of . green that has such a beneficial effect on the ' eyes and affords an admirable protectioh from . the glare of the sea and sun. As will be remern- bered sunshades atg now being carried by a few extremists on the other side of the Channel while bathing, and it is safe to prophesy. ere the season is over that these en tout ca s will be ex- ceedingly popular. They are ,made of pretty waterproofed silk or lingerie trimmed with frills of foamy white lace and aro fitting accompani- ments for the black silk Directoire, bathing cos- tumes that are so fashionable at the French and i other continental seaside resorts.

===== 0002601_19080822_art0013.txt | included=true | included =====
VOILE AFTERNOON DRESS,

Our sketch shows a delightfully cool-looking afternoon dress of vthite cotton voile spotted with green. The full skirt is gathered at. the back, and the hem is trimmed with two rows of white insertion, one above the hem and the other above the tuck. The bodice is finely tucked, and trimmed with crossed bands of white insertion. The softly-draped bands of the

voffe an arranged in pleats, and bordered with lace insertion at each edge. The yoke ,is of white embroidery, or sprigged lace could be used instoad, the ,sleeves being of the same material. finished at the elbow with an up-and-down frill divided by a baud of• insertion. Round the waist is worn a belt of black ribbon velvet, knotted at the side, and the ends finished with balls of velvet.

GARDENING FOR WOMEN.

Gardening—and more especially market-gar- dening— as an occupation for women, should more commend itself to tho consideration of those 'seeking agreeable and lucrative work for girls now that the system of French gardening is claiming so much attention. Hitherto we English folk have not been able to compete with the French, who have supplied our markets with all the early vegetables we use. But now that it id discovered how very easy it is to, produce salads and vegetables even in the winter, market-gardening ought to be taken up, declares the Lady's Pictorial, by women who like coun- try and outdoor life generally. It is a very suit- able occupation. and it is one requiring but small capital. Furthermore, it is to the advan- tage of the nation that this industry should be increased.

===== 0002601_19080822_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN AND THE AIOTOR.

The motor-car of the twentieth century, de- clares Women's Employment, is a marvel of in- genuity, a daintily-equipped fnechanism, easy to handle and to steer, worthy the judgment and resourcefulness of the modern maiden to mani- pulate, to whom this new means of locomotion may become a fascinating pastime and a profit- able profession.

===== 0002601_19080822_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
DRESS FOR A LITTLE GIRL.

The little girl's dress shown in our sketch could be carried out in almost any material, but for present wear would be very nice in some pretty coloured zephyr, or carried out in all white, relieved by a gay-coloured tic, the white cotton hat being trimmed with a scarf to match. The bodice part is simply arranged with two

deep tucks over each shoulder, stitched down as far as the belt. The skirt is slightly gathered all round, and has a tuck above tho hem. The belt is shown in the sketch made of the zephyr and fastened ,by a small tab, to match those that hold down the ends of the tie. Beneath the kimono sleeves there are little puff sleeves of white embroidery, the turn-down collar being of the same material.

===== 0002601_19080822_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
A GIPSY OF HIGH DEGREE.

Lady Arthur Grosvenor, the still youthful- looking aunt of the Duke of Westminster, has just returned from what seems to have been an ideal. caravanning tour through the leafy coun- ties of England. When her Ladyship takes to the road she does so in no frivolous or ama- teurish spirit, says the Sketch, for, accompanied by a friend, she strips herself for the nonce of all. the insignia of rank and gentle birth, and assumes the garb, the colouring, the very accents of the daughter of a Romany; and she is very proud of the fact that so far no one has ever challenged her being what she seems to be. Lady Arthur, who is a keen sportswoman and an intense lover of country life, is mistress of a charming old-world manor-house, the Old Hall, Braxton, Cheshire. She first became the happy owner of a caravan some years ago, and she con- siders tlat there is no more delightful way of spending a holiday than in this leisurely fashion.

===== 0002601_19080822_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
TO-DAY'S RECIPE.

CREME ArX MERLANS. - Clean and split two whitings, and place them in a baking dish in the oven, laying a small piece of butter on the top of each. After ten minutes remove, separate the flesh from the bones and skin, and pound tho fish first in a mortar and then rub through a sieve. Melt half an ounce of gelatine in a little water, and stir it.into half a cupful of fresh tomato puree, seasoned with salt and pepper. Whip half a pint of double cream stiffly, and mix with a tablespoonful of thick mayonnaise sauce. Take a gill of savoury aspic jelly, mask a border mould with half of it. add the remainder to the whipped cream, and stir all into the tomato puree. 'Add the whiting—when quite cold—last of all, and fill the mould with the mix- ture. Set on ice till required, and then turn out. nil the centre with' chopped lettuce, garhialmd pith dim of tpulato mti hard-boiled egg(

===== 0002601_19080822_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
SUBMARINE PHOTOGRAPHY.

Although more than 1,000 species of fish that inhabit the waters of the deep see have been found, there dciubtless remain to be re- vealed rare specimens, unique' in form and colouring. Experiments in submarine Photo- graphy have been made of late years which promise great aid to the work of deep sea fishing. A specially-constructed camera was carried -down by a diver in the employ of the Government to a depth of 22ft. or 23ft., and with an exposure of half, an hour negatives were obtained which, though shewing a lack of depth, were fairly satisfactory. It was found that the best results were obtained by placing a blue glass in front of the lens. It is intended later to have lenses specially con- structed for under-water-work.

===== 0002601_19080822_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
CRIME AND THE THERMOMETER.

The marked .increase in.crimes of violence during hot weather seems to lend support to the theory that crime increases as the ther- mometer rises. A few years ago Mr. Edwin Grant Dexter, an American scientist, pre- pared a chart shewing the relation between crime and the thermometer, as tested by seven years' records. This chart represents the maximum of criminal assaults as occur- ring at a temperature of from 80deg. t 6 85deg. Fahr. With the fall of the tempera- ture the

===== 0002601_19080822_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
CRIMINAL'S, ENERGY DECREASES,

reaching its average at 551eg. to 60deg. Fahr., and its lowest point at from sdegv. to 10deg. Fahr. It is interesting to note that, according to Mr. Dexter's observations, periods of the most intense heat are as free from crime as periods of extreme cold. Other instructite conclusions are that excessive humidity is as productive of crime as ex- treme heat; that low atmospheric pressure preceding a storm keeps the policeman busy; and that women are far more sensitive than men to changes of temperature.

===== 0002601_19080822_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
DURATION OF GROWTH AND LIFE.

Professor Rubner, of Berlin, has made a special study of problems relating to develop- ment and growth, and the Hospital sum- marises some of his deductions and gene- ralisations. The weight of the newly-born creature is doubled by growth in very dif- ferent intervals of time in different animals. The newly-born kitten doubleS its weight in nine days, the calf in forty-seven days, while the human infant requires 180 days. The slow growth of the human body is perhaps compensated by the

===== 0002601_19080822_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
GREATER DEVELOPMENT OF =Am.

I Man has, moreover, an exceptional position in regard to the duration of life and its rela- tion to the letidth of the period of growth. Whilst the horse lives thirty-five years, the cow thirty years, and the dog fifteen years, periods of growth are respectively five, four, and two years—that is, from one-sixth to one- seventh of the total duration of life. In man Ha total duration of life of seven* to eighty ' years is associated with a period of growth , Of twenty to twenty-four years—that is, one- third or one-fourth.

PICTURES FROM THE EYES.

A well-known scientist gives an extra- ordinary account of some experiments on images in the eyes. He believes hhas proved not only that real images, of objects looked at are formed on the retina of the eye, but that photographs ,of such images can be made. In the first instance the experimenter fixed his eyes intently for one minute, in or- dinary daylight, on a two-shilling piece. Then, drawing a yellow screen over the win- dow to shut out the actinic light, he put a prepared photographic plate in place of the two-shillings, and fastened his eyes upon it,

THINKING ALL THE WHILE OF THE COIN. After forty-three minutes he removed the plate and developed it. He asserts that he found an outline of the piece of money clearly visible on the plate. He repeated the experiment with a postage stamp, and, after twenty minutes' staring at a sensitised plate, found on the latter, when it was developed, an image of the stamp. -The light producing the alleged photographs is supposed to emanate from the image on the retina.

TO MEASURE HUMAN INTELLIGENCE.

Dr. Van Biervliet, the Dutch psychologist, I has, after long years of research, come to the conclusion that the intelligence can be, mea- sured, and that because it depends upon mental application and Affection, its " vari- ant " can be found among all sorts and con- ditions of men. Hero is the argument he advances in support of his novel theory: The most complex intellectual operations are all reducible to a ratiocinative process Ohich depends for the results it gives on the soundness of the nervous system and its fine- ness. You cannot, for example, get from a 'cello the effects which are to be obtained from the violin, the reason being explained by the fineness of thd strings. Although in their specific qualities the notes extracted from either instrument may be supreme, there can be no queseon that in fameaehingeffect that of the more delicate i . instrument immeasurably exceeds the other. An almost identical process takes place with the mind, the superior mental organism being that which is composed of most nerve- cells, or, as we should say, which fs post highli'r strung. In projecting its attentioTl or ratiocinative power upon any, given question which requires reasoning out, the will simply calls upon its SUPPLY OF CEREBRAL NERVE-CELLS.

Naturally, says the New York World, the more there are of thesa the greater. and more effective will be the " training " of the intel- ligence.upon the subject under consideration. Now, a fairly approximate gauge of the 1 power of the nervous .system can be obtained either from the 6harpneps of hearing or the , sharpness of sight, says Van Biervliet. Take) a dozen intelligent persona, and you will find that the most intelligent among them are those who best apply their senses of hearing and seeing, the sense of 'touch being about equal in all normally - conditioned persons. -The effect of a given incident upon a spec- tator, or of a statement upon a hearer, can be estimated from the number of images which are created in the sensorial apparatus. In' the mind which is capable of most con- centration, and, consequently, capable of exerting the, most intelligence, these images will succeed each other in a logical order, and will be all closely related to each other. In the mind which is least intelligent, and in which the

WOMEN'S CHAT.

Royal Visitcrs from Spain—Princess Henry of Vette-rib/ill, like the late Queen Victoria, is fond of the Isle of Wight, where she is staying It Osborne Cottage, and has with her the Queen of Spain, and also her eldest son Prince Alexandre, who has retired from the Navy on account of his delicate health. Queen Victoria Eugenie, who is in need of rest and quiet, was greatly upset by the assassiration of the King and Crown Prince of Portugal, ard her nerves Lave suffered from the constant apprehension of danger. Contrary to expectation she did not bring her two children, but it is expected that the Queen will be joined by King Alfonso, who proposes to spend a few days in the Isle of Wight early next month. Under the Spanish constitution, the Ring and Heir Apparent cannot be absent from the country at the same time, and the King cannot leave Spain withont the formal consent of his Parliament. Formerly a similar statutory rule prevailed in this i country,but it has fallen into desuetnde.The last time the question was raised was in ISIS, when Queen Victoria was about to visit Clermsny. Lord Campbell urged that the Lords Justices should be commissioned to act during the Sovereign's abs.,nce, but the Lord Chancellor con- vinced the House of Lords that this formality was no longer necessary, in consequence of the greatly improved means of travel and telegraphic communication. A precedent was thus established which did away with these old-time formalities. A Royal Lover of Huge Headgear.—lt is abundantly evident that the young Queen of Spain has a great affection for very large hats, which so far during her stay in this country have been surrounded by sweeping ostrich plumes, or ornamented with huge wings. For the " Toby " ruffle, ,or the chiffon scarf, her Majesty apparently cares not at, all, her favourite ostrich feather boas—of which she possesses quite a wonderful collection—being worn upon every possible occasion. So far, we have failed to discover any neck and shoulder " fluish," to compare—in becomingness- -with the ostrich feather boa, which, happily, will be worn as much as ever when the cooler days arrive. Button to the Fore.—Very little has so far transpired noncerning early autumn fashions, but of this we may rest assurred that buttons will abound upon smart toilettes, indeed not infrequently modistes will rely entirely upon them for decoration. Quite small speci- mens are likely to be preferred, and these will be placed very eh sely together. Plaids Again.—The plaid season has arrived and the most artistic mixtures are being offered in shopland. All-plaid costumes, however, are less seen this yesr than formerly, many tailors preferring to mix the plaids with striped fabrics. For example, a striped material, closely pleated, is used for the skirt, one broad band of the plaid appearing at the foot, the coat of the striped fabric showing revers, pocket flaps, and cuffs of the plaid also. Felt hats trimmed with velvet keipds and quills or wines, are worn with these costumes. Keeping Birds in Cages.—People are so accustomed to the idea of keeping song birds in cages that few ever stop to think how much cruel suffering is occasioned by this practice. Women are charged with vanity and thoughtlessness for wearing the skins and feathers of birds in this hats, but, in this respect it might well be asked whether the milliner's shop does not represent an insufficient measure of cruelty and suffering com- pared with the live-stock shop, crowded from floor to calling with cages, whose occupants were made to soar, but are doomed to life-long captivity. The same objection does not perhaps apply to canaries and other birds which never have known freedom, but no living thing likes captivity, and there is a certain amount of selfish cruelty in keeping birds in cages, even if their wants are regularly and properly cared for, which is very far from always being the case. Are Holidays a Mistake.—The newspapers having dis- cussed the topic "What is the best form of holiday " are now giving es a dissertation on the question "Are holidays any good ? " An eminent French medical authority says they are altogether a mistake. He says we do not want long rests, and that to relieve a jaded mind by going in for abnormal physical exercise in the holidays, only makes matters worse. The cccasional day, or hstf-day outing he suggests, is much better than the three or four weeks summer holiday from which, he goes on to say nine out of ten persons return all the worse, and are unable to resume work properly for two or three days afterwards. All this of course, is con- trary to the general belief. It is quite true that a great many people do return the worse for their holidays, but they start, not with any idea of benefiting their health, but with the sole oblect of crowding as ranch pleasure as is possible into the time they are away. If one is done up with ill-health or overwork, it is too much to ask us to believe that the occasional half-day holiday is better than the long rest and change. The Elder Sisters' Burdens.—Some evidence bearing on the question whether women or men have the best time in life, is afforded by the way in which the children spend their days in the streets and parks of the great cities and towns, when the board schools are closed for the summer holidays. While the boys play , games, the. girls for the most part are turned into I nursemaids. The boys play at motor cars, in the form of sugar-Loxes on wheels, but the girl's are placed in charge of the perambulator, and are made responsible for the care of two or three or perhaps more younger children. It is the girls and not the boys who most frequently ask the passing stranger for the " right time" because the ~hours pass slowly and they are 1 longing for the time to come to go home fur dinner or i tea. The boys seem to have very much the best of it in holiday time, and in this early training there is little of the chivalry, which we are told the stronger sex used to extend to the weaker, but which moralists tell us, women have forfeited the right to expect from men, since they have sought to share the opportunities and enjoyments of life on equal tering with them. Mr. Churchill's Engagement.—The forthcoming wedding of Mr. Winston Churchill, President of the Board of Trade, and Miss Clementine Hozier, daughter , of the late Sir Henry Hozier, will be the fourth Minis- ' terial marriage within it year. Mr. McKenna, and Mr. Masterman, married in June, while Lord Lorebtirn vras nserried to Miss Violet Hicks-Beach last December. It is understood that the engagement be Mr. Churchill and Miss Hozier will be a very short one, and that the wedding will take place next month. Tall and slight, Miss Hozier is regarded as one of the cleverest and most agreeable girls in Society, and besides being good- looking, she adds accomplishments of a musical, literary, and artistic kind, such as are attaired by few young ' ladies of her age. She is twenty-three, some ten years younger than Mr. Winston Churchill, whose career, yet in its early days, has teen marked by so many notable achievements. As the wife of Mr. Winston Churchill, the prospective bride will be able to turn her splendid talents to full account, and the morriage will give the President el the Board of Trsde, a matrimonial, as well as a political alliance with Forfarshire, owing to the near relationship of Miss Hozier with the Airlie family. She is a niece of the late Earl of Airlie, who was killed in the Boeis War, and whose seat, Cortachy Castle, is situated some 15 or 16 miles from Dundee. MADGE.

SNOW FALLS AT THE EQUATOR.

If there is one place in the world that we cannot associate with snowstorms it is the Equator or its vicinity. But the exploring ex- pedition ,of Duke Adolf Frederick of Ger- znany, in the volcanic region of German East Africa, was overtaken by a terrific snow- storm, which stopped their progress in the crater of Branca—about ninety miles south of the Equator, half his men being frozen to death.

PHOTOGRAPHIC NOTES.

By Veronica.

BREVITIES.

The International Geographical Congress which has just completed its session inilllGeneva has appointed a special commision to make a collection of photographs of all the various forms of the earth's surface. FRANCO-BRITISH AT NIGHT. Amateur photographers are now allowed to work in the Franco-British ExhiLition on condition of giving an undertaking that their results will not be used for commercial purposes. One of the men who has made very good use of this permission is Mr. Robert Dykes author of" Night Photography," from vrhora I have recently seen some very interesting prints from nega- tives made in the twilight with the lighting of the illuminations, but before the daylight was com- pletely gone. No doubt many of my readers have copies of Mr. Dykes' book and will be glad of the suggestions to follow his example. GOOD WORK WITH CHEAP OUTFITS. The excellent work that can be done by anyone who specialises, who is careful, and keeps within the limita- tion of his outfit, is often a source of surprise even to an old photographer. I have just seen a set of half a dczen flower studies, all made with a as. camera, and by an amateur who only began work this year. Ills success is due, partly to his care, and no doubt, partly also, to his love for flowers FIRST STEPS IN DEVELOPING. Many of my readers who are new to photography are probably coming back from their holidays with exposed plates and films and are very anxious to develop them I to the best advantage. If they can do no I should strongly advise them to have the assistance of a practical demonstration by some good photographer. I This would tend to give them confidence in the dark- room. Thereafter I advise them to make one or two I 'exposures on objects that need not have any interest, simply for the sxke of having unimportant plates on which to commence practising. Start with these, using ready made developer, either procured in solution from the chemist, or in tablet or tabloid form, which only requires solution in water. THE ACTUAL DEVELOPMENT.

Lay the plate in the developing dish with the face (film) upward and the glass side down. Do not soak the plate in water before pouring on the developer,altllough this course was advised by many of the earlier land- books,and is still practised by some go e &photographers. Be sure that the quantihy of-developer is sufficient to cover the plate completely as it lies in the dish. If there is any doubt about this, test the matter by using the same quantity of eater and flowing in over the dry negative or, if no resat 7ve is available, over the plate you are going to develop. %Then flowing over the developer take the dish in the left hard whh the thumb over one top corner and thellngers spread under. the dish. Take measure containing the developer in the right hand, begin pouring it steadily cn to the right hand end of the for side of the plate moving the measure plonk to, the left so that the 'last of the develeper is poured on the, left end of the plate. This will cover the whole of the farther half of the plate and a slight tilt of the dish tovcards yourself should cause the solution to flow evenly ocer all parts. It is important the developer should be flowed evenly in this way or the negative will show what are known as developer marking. No hairy or splash is needed and the whole of the developer should be poured on the plate—(not into the corners and edges of the dish so that it runs under the p'ate). In this way air bubbles will be avoided. WATCHING DEVELOPMENT. This is always an anxious time for the young beginner, and he is liable to strain his eyes and to hold his develop- ing dish quite close to the glass of a lamp which may not he perfectly light safe, with the result that he runs some risk of fogging his plate. The best thing to do the moment the developer has been seen to cover the plate completely and without air bubbles, is to cover the dish with another one or with a sheet of card end to leave it completely alone while about a minute is counted in seconds. Then raise the card and look at the film giving the dish a slight rack. The whole of the creamy appea..ince of the film will have appeared to have become slightly grey and probably certain parts will be very appreciably greyer than others. 'Mese are the high lights of the original subject which are to be densetblack in the negative. if this is the condition or if the creamy appearance of the negative is still unaltered, cover the dish again and take another look at the end of another minute. Eventually it will be seen that the higher lights are becoming black and that the middle tones of the Picture are developing to a general grey, while some of the shadow points shbuld still remain perfectly creamy, end will perhaps, by con' rest, look a lighter colour than the whole film did when first examined. WHEN TO STOP DEVELOPMENT.

Let the development continue s4pi an occasional slight rock of the dish (continuous rocking is no advantage but rather the rrerse) until it is seen that detail is beginning to be shown even in the darkest parts, unless these ere so colnpletely unlighted in the original as to need representing by absolute black in the print. When it seems as if there is some darkening of the film almost, or absolutely, all over, it is well to take out the negative and have a look at the glass side. It sill then be seen that the under side of the film (now visible from the glass) has developed more slowly than the upper side which was the first to be attacked by the developer, and that therefore more detail and contrast remains visible on the glass side. Almost all amateurs make the mistake of stopping development too soon, and I suggest that they should always carry it forward until the detail, with the exception of the very deepest shadows, is all lost from the glass side.

-This was tie right topic as far as he was con- cerned, for he had come to talk to Mrs. Brownei with the vague hope that she might say some- thing which would throw light on Margaret's strange behaviour that evening. and at once encountered 'Ralph, who was lookin for her.

"I think he is rather nice."

When Jack arrived to call on the Fairfaxes next day, he was informed that Nell was the only person at home, and was straightway ushered into her presence. "I havoc brought back your ring," he said, sit- ting down in a chair beside her.

She held out her hand for it, but he slipped the ring on her little finger himself instead. Even when that was done he did not 'relinquish the' hand. "I'm .going away to-morrow for six months,!". he said. She turned her head away from him, and did not speak. When at last she looked round again her eyes were full of tears. FOR HER SAKE.

13? G. EDWARD BEARD.

Authcr of " Nellamay," "The Doctor's Patient," "The Ro na.nco of a Deception," -" The Pro- fessor's Masterpiece,:' "Twenty per Cent.," "In the Merry Xmas Tim" &c.

"No! no! no!" and the girl looked up 4ith flushed face and resolute eyes. "Why will you continually revert to this subject, Mr. Stallard, when I have so repeatedly given you my definite answer? I, have hitherto been pleased to meet you as a friend—l have been grateful to you for all the many kindnesses you have shewn to me and my mother—but I do not love you, and shall never be able to return the affection which you profess for me in the way you wish. Cannot you understand that this is, my final and irrevocable answer?"

The young man, who stood on the other side of the gate over which she was leaning, flashed a look of mingled disappointment and fury at her, as he answered hotly: "I know why you re- fuse me! That mealy-mouthed, non-smoking, teetotal sneak, Martin—my father's bailiff—is your beau ideal! I know a, Rose! You think more of him than you do of me. Goodness knows why; but you do. But, curse him, he shall never marry you!"

"Stay, stay, Mr. Stallard! Hoyt dare you? Mr. Martin has never made Iny profession of more than ordinary friendliness for me—never— and you have no right to couple my name with his in suclv a manner. If he neither smokes nor drinks he has at least a civil tongue in his head, and I don't think he would lower himself by calling you a 'mealy-mouthed sneak.' Good evening, Mr. Stallard "—and the girl turned to- wards the house.

Before she had gone many steps away, his voice again arrested her. "Rose! Misl Isreen! Don't go like that. Don't treat me so coldly. I love you desperately—with my whole heart. I'll do anything you can ask me—anything—to win you. Cannot you give me a little love? Oh! -Rose, if. you only knew how I adore you! With you as my wife everything would be changed. I would settle down on the estate, and be a model husband. You should choose my companions for me. My father would be made the happiest old man alive, and I—with you as my wife, reigning as mistress up at the Hall—life would be to me a dream of delight, and I would make you so happy, Rose, and would strive my hardest to prove worthy of you, dear. Won't you give me just a 'little hope?" - The young man's usually florid face grew pale in the) intensity of his passion, and he gazed with pleading eyes at the little figure that stood on the garden path. She came a step nearer to the gate, regarding him with firmness, not unmixed with pity. "I can never change my mind, Mr. Stallard," she said. "It would be simply cruel and wrong to hold out hopes of what can never be. I am sorry if I wound your feelings, but I can never love you—never; and I have never knowingly encouraged you to think ot me otherwise than as a friend. That is all I can say. And now again geed-night I" and she turned and entered the house.

For some seconds Reuben Stallard stood star* mg at the door through which she had dis- appeared, then, with a dark and angry scowl/ on his otherwise handsome features, he clenched( his fist and hurried away. Left to herself, Rose Kreen, finding her widowed mother asleep in the armchair, Dung herself down on a sofa, and indulged in reflective consideration.

How strangely crooked Fate had made affairs to work in her life. Here was Reuben Stallard— rollicking, loose-lived, rich and handsome Reuben—for ever at her side professing his affec- tion for her, and begging for a little of that love which she knew in her heart was wholly given to Will Martin, the steady, matter-of-fact, busi- ness-like young bailiff on the Hall estate; and he—Will Martin—beyond evincing evident plea- sure in her society had made no sign—given no visible token of an answering emotion. Once or twice she had thought that she had detected a lock in his eyes—but there! he had made no fur- ther advances, and Leap Year though it was, Rose Kreen had too much . pride and maidenly reserve in her nature to make her feelings known un- asked. . . .

And so matters stood. She had again striven to convince Reuben that it was, and ever would be, useless for him to repeat his declarations of love, and Will Martin was silent. Either he feared to risk his fate by an open avowal, or she had been mistaken about those momentary gleams that came into his dark 'yes, and, after all, he had no warmer feeling 'for her than friend- ship. ,Ah I well, time' alone would shew. Any- how, she must ever remain true to her own heart, and to the love that dwelt in secret there. Meanwhile, she had her life to live, her duties to perform, and her mother to comfort and cherish: She would do these loyally and leave the rest to God.

The summer was over, and the tints of autumn were beautifying the woods and hedgerows around. Rose Kreen had seen little of Will Mar- tin of late. Squire Stallard had discovered that in his new overseer he had gained a treasure, the value of which he was appreciating fully as time went on.

The son of well-to-do parents in a distant part of the country, he had accepted his present sub- ordinate position to gain praetical experience of different styles of farming and stock-keeping from those which prevailed in the neighbourhood from which he had come, and bringing a large amount of intelligence and sound common sense ,to bear on all the duties he undertook, he became in- 'valuable to the squire, who entrusted to him the entire supervision of the extensive changes and improvements that lie was having made on his various outlying farms. And so it happened that his time being thus fully occupied he had but seldom met Rose, and if on those rare occasions she had sometimes ven- tured to ,think that his' glances conveyed someL thing more than mere friendly admiration, no word had escaped his lips, and so it was still a matter of mere conjecture on her part—this pos- sible love for which her heart yearned. As for Reuben Stallard, he had apparently taken his last rebuff as final and had not again approached her on the subject.

The girl was committsd to Holy Trinity fndestrial School, Liverpool, on the order of two magistrates. She was at the time of her cAmmittal an inmate of the Cottage Homes at Padgate. THE BISHOP'S SISTER-IN- LAW.

BY MRS. WILLIE STEVENSON,. • Author of "A- Candle Lamp Adventure," &a.

The Bishop sat sunning himself on the bal- cony. What a beautiful scene lay before him—the blue Mediterranean! Where is i there sea so cerulean, or land where one can feel so thoroughly idle as the South of is belle France? The exhilarating rays of sun and sea, in mid:winter, had had the tonic effect that the Bishop's doctor had promised. By the end of next week he would be in the midst of his beloved diocesan work once more. I Meantime—but his reveries were inter- rupted by the entrance of Nora Vining, his I charming sister-in-law. She was buttoning I the last button of %dainty pair of gloves. In answer to his inquiring glance, ," I am just going for a drive with Mr. and Mrs. Black," she said. "Tell Margaret I shall be back in time for dinner."

A few minutes after the Bishop's wife ap- peared on the balcony. "Nora has gone for a drive with Mr.. and Mrs. Black, my dear." "I hope they will -not drive to Monte Carlo," said the Bishop's wife.

"Why not, my dear?" It is the most lovely drive about here." . •

Mrs'. G— did not answer. She was glad her husband was of an entirely unsuspicious nature. She knew full well that this lively sister of hers did not feel bound by all the environments of a Bishop. "You are the Bishop's wife, dear old Margaret—Rot I. You will not be held accountable for what / do," she was wont to say when remonstrated with. about some rather harum-scarum pro- ceeding. Mrs. G— had an uneasy feeling that if Nora came in the neighbourhood of the Casino without the restraining presence of the Bish9p she would enter therein. , Meantime Nora, with Mr. and Mrs. Black, had driven off, as Mrs. G— rightly guessed, to Monte •Carlo.

" I am glad you are going to Monte Carlo. You will take me to the Casino, will you, not, Mrs. Black?" said Nora, in her most per- suasive tones.

"We always go to the gardens to hear the music. Ido not think we must take her in the Casino itself. What do you say, George?" said Mrs. Black to her husband.

"Well, my dear, my difficulty is to keep you out of the Casino when we go to Monte Carlo."

Nora clapped her hands, and exclaimed, "Oh, hqw lovely! What would the Bishop and Margaret say if they only knew! You will not tell them will you, Mr. 131ack? It is so terribly humdrum, having a Bishop for a brother-in-law. We have been two whole months here, and have only driven through Monte Carlo. To be in the Riviera, without going to the Casino at Monte Carlo is like going to Genoa without seeing its palaces, or to Venice without going in a gondola, or— oh dear! what shall I say;" and Nora stopped for breath. . She was on the point of saying "without seeing St. Mark's," but pulled up in time, thinking it not quite A propos.

"But yeu will net tell the Bishdp, will you, Mr. Black?" said she again, anxiously.

"We shall have to be careful it does not slip out some time," said he, teasing, her. The drive was superb. Nora chatted gaily all the way. Arrived at the Casino, Mr. Black led his, wife and Nora into the Sallee de Jeu. Nora felt considerable qualms of conscience, as she noticed the free-and-easy way in which people went backwards and forwards through the •doore. Supposing the Bishop's sister-in-law were recognised. Sho would not like to reflect any discredit on her brother-in-law, of whose unworldliness she was secretly rather proud. Through the doors they passed, and were soon in one of the Salles de Jeu. What a gay scene it was, with the flashing jewels, the brilliant costumes of the women and men walking -up and down the spacious hall! A few moral reflections .rushed through her mind. Not much use coming here when your money was spent. HA- , sad that young Mr. J—at their hotel had lost his money gaming here, and had thrown •himself from the rocks close by. But she' wes in no mood to give such thoughts as these house-room, and, guided by Mr. Black, she made her way to one of the 'tables. Around, were seated those possessed with the gambler's passion. Among the gay outer throng she caught sight of a most curiously-dressed young man. Surely an Englishman, and a gentleman by his bearing. And yeti And yet! Such a costume! Light tweed coat and waistcoat; navy-blue trousers. Was ever Englishman abroad in such garb before? For, a moment she was convulsed with -laughter. She was about to draw Mrs. Black's attention to the odd •costume, when his eyes- were arrested by hers. Noting her amusement, his, too, answered back full of merriment. In no mood for even a flirtation' of the eyes, in such a place, and with such a being, the Bishop's sister-in-law drew herself up haugh- tily, and proceeded to watch the game.

After a little time, Nora cried- excitedly, "Mr. Black, do put this on for me!" at the same time handing him a five.frane piece. "I must try what it is like for my- self." '

Though no gamester, Mr. Black -held a sort of happy-go-lucky theory that pleasure was one thing, duty another. He divided his in- come into two parts—so much for duty, ,so much' for pleasure. If a portion of pleasure money were spent on gambling, why not? He could stop in time. He dii not stay to reflect on the effect such a' course might have on a less phlegmatic nature than his own, and moss particularly on one so impulsive as Nora. He put the money down for her; and again and again she won, until even he thought she had .had enough, and managed to draw her away. As they were getting into the , carriage, Nora's attention was arrested by that pecu- liar young man watching them again. ' There was . something fascinating about him. - Had he pawned his clothes, she .wondered. He must be short of money to be dressed so. "Perhaps my gain was his loss," thought Nora, conscience already beginning, to prick, now that she was away from the gambling atmosphere. -

_ Mr. Black's inward voice must also have been at work, for on the drive home he said, Misi-.Ncra, if I promise you not to tell the Bishop, will you -give me a promise?" "What is it? " "That you never play again." by? ' • "Because it is most dangerous for a be- ginner to win as you hate eon." "I can readily premise.. We start for home the day after to-morrow, and I am not likely to be in the neighbourhood, of a casino for years and years. Basides, when I think of poor Mr. ,J— I feel I shall never •,-ant to 'play any More." Curious that Mr. J---'s suicide had not produced this effect earlier.

"Whatever shall I do with this money? I shall feel wicked, I know, in the presence of that brother-in-law of mine, until I get rid of it. I have a brilliant idea. I will have a farewell tea party at K—'s restaurant to- morrow. You will come, Mrs. Black, will you not? and. -Mr. Black? and I will ask the Bishop and Margaret. Dop't tell the Bishop where the money has come from!" said she once more, as she got out of the carriage. "Don't forget your promise to ,me," said Mr, Black, as she ran up ' the steps of the hotel.

In the garden of K—'s restaurant next day, a merry party were sitting at bee. The conversation was kept as much as possible on people staying at the hotel, or on home topics. The drive of yesterday was avoided by the common consent of three of the party, at least. The genial Bishop was, as usual, first-rate raconteur, and had an attentive audience. tie en3oyea ins tea, remarking afterwards to his wife that " Nora's coal shares must be doing well. She would insiet on treating us all to that itimptuoui tek:k

Mrs. 03, kept her own counsel. Nil did she ever inquire where and how Nora hat spent the afternoon with Mr. and Mrs. Black.

As for Nora, she had been looking about most of the time for the oddly-dressed young man. But she saw him no more. Next day they started for home.

"Dean Martin and his son are coming to lunch to-day, Nora," said, Mrs. G—, a. Nora was going out one morning, some • months after their visit to the Riviera.

I will be home in

"All right, Margaret. good time," said Nora. Guests were constantly coming and going at the Bishop's Palace. When Nora came into the drawing-room, before lunch, all aglow from a game of hockey in which she had just been taking part, she was intro- duced to Dean Martin and his son Jack. The latter, it appeared, was recently home from walking the hospitals. Dean Martin himself had only lived in the dioceSe a few months. As she acknowledged the introduc- tion, Nora was annoyed to see Jack Martin's eyes full of merry laughter. What cause was there for amusement? And yet! Where had she seen those eyes before? She moved away to a far window.,, He fol- lAed her to admire the view.

"Yes, is it not lovely from here?" she said, in answer to his comment. "Rather a different style from what we saw when we met before,' said he. "Met before ! Where have We met? ". sra Nora, somewhat haughtily. "At Monte Carlo."

" Hush !>, said Nora, turning very pale. "Then you are the curiously-dressed English. man I noticed there. We must talk about this in the garden after lunch, but not a word at lunch. No one here knows I have been to Monte Carlo," said she, hurriedly, in a low voice, as they went into the dining. room.

During lunch, Nora was absorbed in her own thoughts. What manner of man was this Jack Martin? Ho • looked handsome and gentlemanly enough now. But the son of a Dean, to be seen at Monte Carlo, in bor- rowed clothes ! They must have been bor- rowed. He must be a gambler; and yet he seemed rather nice. Did his father know, she wondered. She thought not; for in the midst of her _abstraction she overheard the Dean speaking in quite confidential tones to the Bishop of the splendid position in the medical world that had just been offered to his son. Jack was engaged in conversation with Margaret at the other end of the table. Then Nora reflected on her own sins, as she tried to picture, what Jack's impression of the Bishop's sister-in-law putting money on the gaming table had been. All things come to those who wait. At last the lunch was over. All turned involun- tarily to the open window, and so out into the garden. Nora said to her sister, " Shall I shew Mr. Martin through the green- houses?"

" Certainly," said Mrs. G—. "Come back in time for coffee. We shall have it on the lawn." As soon as they were out of hearing, Nora turn rea to Mr. Martin, and said in the severest tones she could command, "Now tell me how the son of a Dean came to be at Monte Carlo." "May I asit how the sister-in-law of a Bishop was seen to be gaming at the same place?" • "Hush ! Don't say that," said Nora, pleadingly. "I would not have the Bishop know for worlds."

Nora, in common with the rest of man- kind, shared the belief that "two blacks ought to make a white." She therefore preferred to keep the conversation on the re- flection of Jack's sins (which must be the greater), rather than turn the microscope on her own. Her visit to Monte Carlo would not have been in vain if, by acting father confessor to this nice young man, she could turn him from his wicked gambling ways. 'You must have lost all your money to have been in those borrowed clothes," she said, with the air of a Mentor. • "Borrowed clothes ! " laughed 'Jack, heartily. "They were not borrowed; they were my own. I have them yet. They are historic. I am thinking of presenting them to the local museum. One of the most ex- pensive suits I ever had!" "I suppose you mean they were expensive because you lost so much meney at the table," said Nora. _

"Is that worse than to have won money as I saw somebody doing?" Nora coloured. "Perhaps not. But • you did look funny. I wish I had taken a snap- shot of you," she said, laughing. "Supposing I have taken a snap-shot of you ? " said he. "How cruel! No you haven't." "Yes I have. I had my hand camera with me. I took a photo, just as you were getting into the carriage. You remember you got, in from the far side. Here it is." And from his pocket he pulled out, somewhat shyly, a dainty leather case, containing a photo of herself. Now, when a woman rinds that a man has been carrying her photo about in his pocket for months, unknown to herself, she naturally feels that he is a man of some discrimination •and taste. Her heart warms to him abeordingly. Nora dropped the severe tone she had felt proper to , assume with this young gambler, and said, softly, "Tell me all about it." - "I will, after you have told me how you came to be at Monte Carlo."

Nora related her story as briefly as she could. How she had longed to go, whenever they had driven through the place. The fact of her relationship to the Bishop made the longing all the greater. She dare not mention it to her own folks. She described how Mr. and Mrs. Black, who were friends she had made at the hotel, took her for a drive, and consented to chaperon her in the Casino. 'When there, she could not resist pitting money on the tables. Then she told how, catching his eye, she feared from his odd dress that he must have, lost heavily; and, after, that, she had made a solemn vow never to gamble for even the smallest sum again. You will promise me, will you not, to do the same?" said she, earnestly turning to him.

1"I am sorry to refuse you anything. But -I am afraid I cannot."

"Why not? " said • Nora, , beginning t• freeze again._

"Because I have never begun to gamble yet:" _ _

• What were you doing at Monte Carle, then, in such a guise?" At this moment, a maid came towards them, with a message from Mrs. G—, say- ing that the coffee was getting cold. As soon as Jack had finished his coffee, Dean Martin rose to go. Jack had perforce to make his adieus too.

Nora stamped her little foot impatiently. Just to leave the story in its most exciting part. It was too tantalising! In shakint hands with Jack she contrived to whisper, "I must see you again. Call to-morrow at 3.30." •

ivtith them for a month. At the end of that time I was going on to San Reino and Genoa, intending to make my way up home through the Italian Lakes and Switzerland. "I registered my luggage, and sent it on to San Remo. This being done, I found I Could spare three or four hours at Monte Carlo, on the way, and have a look in at the Casino. I knew that all one had to do was to present one's card at the entrance. This I accordingly did. was about to • pass through the doors, when it was politely ex- plained in French, by the door-keeper, point- ing to my stockings and knickerbockers—for I was wearing a golfing suit—that Monsieur's costume was not comme ii faut, and could not be allowed to pass. "Highly amused at what seemed an ab- surd barrier, I repaired to the one and only tailor in the place. To him I explained my difficulty. He happened to have just one suit, which a young Englishman had ordered and left on his hands. He wanted an exor- bitant price for it. Finally hoping I should see nobody who knew me, and not caring to be beaten in my desire to see the Casino, I bought the nether garments. The result of dark blue below and light tweed above was scarcely a happy combination, as you know. "Changed from a respectable being into a sort of glorified tramp, they allowed me to pass into the gilded saloon, now that my nether garments were the prescribed length. "I was soon nauseated with what I saw. The passion for gambling, the clutching of coins, Faites votre jeu, Mesdames et Mes- sieurs,' had no attraction for me, and I was coming away, when suddenly I caught your eye, and noticed how amused you were at my costume. "Then I saw you hand your five-franc piece to your friends, and the fascination the play was having for you. I recognised you , immediately. I had seen you once in church here. at home before you left for France. We came to this neighbourhood about a month previous to the Bishop's. illness. "More from mischief than anything else, I took a snap-shot of you, thinking how I would surprise you when we met here, as I knew we were bound to do sooner or later. "And now, Nora, I have a confession to . . . .

make. Having had' the photograph with me all these months, I have grown to love the original. lam not the first man to fall in love with a photo. But now I want to have the original always with me. Tell me, is there any chance for a man who means to make his way in the medical world.? It would be so good for me, to have somebody always with me to keep me from gambling, and to set me so excellent an example in the matter," said he, mischievously. "If you will never mention Monte Carlo or breathe the faintest hint as to where you took the photo, there might be a chance some day," said Nora, pensively. "Then why not now?" said Jack, ee took her little hand in his. Azid Nora did not pull it away, / - rrins END.I

COMPULSORY SERVICE. -- Straight Talk by Admiral Sir Cyprian Bridge.

Admiral Sir Cyprian Bridge writes to the "Times " on the question of compulsory service. Ile says that the more closely conscription or compulsory service is examined the more out-of-date and incompatible with modern conditions it will be found. It dates back to a period earlier than that of the immense growth of city populations. As a mere administrative measure for facilitating the work of raising men for defence forces it once had advantages. These were more apparent and the defects were less easily seen when the process of converting communities which were mainly agricultural into communities which were largely " industrial " had not gone very far. When people were scattered tn. small 'separate bodies over a large extent of territory unprovided with the means of rapid communication—which was the condition of Con- tinental Europe at the time—rigid rules of enlistment lightened the recruiters' labours considerably. This justification, such as it was, disappeared when great masses of men were concentrated in cities. The policy of adhering to a.system which takes absolutely no note of a vast revolution in economic conditions has little to recommend it. It is no wonder that SYMPTOMS OF IMPATIENCE regarding compulsory service are to be perceived in all the great military States of Europe. The violent " anti-militarism," occasional outbreaks of which in France and Italy are reported in English newspapers, indicates this impatience in an extreme form. It shows itself also in quieter ways. One of these is the gradu- ally successful effort to reduce the period of compulsory 1"colour service." Dissatisfaction with the German military system accounts largely for the three and a quarter millions of Socialist votes cast at the last general election for the Reichstag. The truth is that the urban democracy—the urban proletariat if you will—has discovered the arcanom imperii, the real secret of the modern military empire. It is 010, if you force men to serve you need pay them only in pence, whereas if you get them to serve voluntarily you must count their pay in shillings. This makes compulsory service extremely agreeable to the well-to-do or "comfortable class." It saves its pocket, to begin with. It is instructive to see how carefully in the 'conscription countries the members of this class guard themselves against the provisions of the com- pulsory service laws which may cause them incon- venience. It is reasonable to believe that many, or indeed, most, thoughtful officers in the great European military States see that

COMPOSORY SERVICE IS OUT OP DATE and incompatible with modern conditions, and that its abolition is slimly, if not rapidly approaching. That it will be adhered to long after it had better be given up is certain. That is the case with many instrtutiods. Compulsory service may be easy to adopt: it is very difficult to get rid of. There will. always be people personally interested in defending and trying to main- tam it. being able, months or years ahead, to tell pretty exactly how many men you can count on does make more agreeable the lives of the worthy gentlemen who instead of serving with the troops or at sea, sit at desks in Ministries of War and Ministries of Marine. The defence of an Empire ought, surely, not be made to turn upon their theory of a quiet life. It has been alleged that the mere fact of compulsion being legalised woald 1111 the ranks without ever putting It into practical operation. This is disproved by our own experience and that of Continental States. The press-gang was, and indeed still is, part of the law of the land.

POINTS OF AGREE:UFA:T. Bearing in mind the eminence of some of those who approve, or at least indirectly lend their support to, the agitation in favour of compulsory service, it is pleasant to think that there are points on which agree- ment with them is possible. These would be the following. It is the duty of everyone to defend his c auntry. Some foreigners hate and envy us so much that they will atteck us as soon as they believe they can do so with success. We must be prepared for this, and be prepared in good time. To be efficient the preparation must comprise proper organisation and training of the men likely to be called upon to take an active part in the defence of the country. I respectfnlly assert that there is no evidence that the people of this country have been reluctant to come forward to defend it. All available evidence tends to establish the con- trarg. It is true that on particular occasions they have come forward later than was desirable. That, however, wasnot their fault ; but the fault of those who omitted to summon them in time. Something very different from compulsory service will be required to rectify want of foresight in a Government Those who take the least favourable view of their fellow-countrymen's courage and patriotism may admit that Englishmen can be got to fight for their homes when danger is visible and near ; but at the same time will assert that nothing short of legal compulsion can make them prepare—that is, submit to training in advance. The entirely sufficient answer to this is that proper measures have not been taken. Men rarely do anything without a motive. If the motive is provided there need be little fear as to the Englishman's prepar- ing himself betimes to meet any enemy that can pluck up courage to attack us. We have got to make it worth the average man's while to undergo a course of training in time of peace. The argument that, because by force of law a portion of a man's wealth is taken from him as taxes, and his children are made to go to school, compulsion to serve in the ranks is admissible, needs little notice. It could be easily disposed of. Let the advocates of conacriptinn—call it by what name they will—understand that, if they succeed in their agitation, they will ruin the recruiting system of the British Nary, which every Continental State longs to have; and that they will gain no compensating advantage to set against that disastrous result.

The Chief Liberal Whip, LIBERAL Mr. J. A. PEASE, has ORGANISATION. informed a correspondent that he is taking counsel with Lord MARCIIIIILEY and Captain CECIL NORTON as to the better organisation of London constitueuces. This is in consequence of the loss of the late Sir RANnALORBITER'S seat at Haggerston, where it was found that though the Conserbative candidate had been nursing the constituency for years past absolutely nothing had been done to keep the Liberal vote together. Nothing could have saved the seat at ifaggerston under the circumstances. Had the Liberal candidate polled a great many more votes than had ever before been recorded for Liberalism in that division of London, he would still have been left far behind, in conse- quence of the candidature of Mr. HERBERT BURROWS. Nevertheless, had, the Liberal forces been organised, it is highly improbable that Mr. RUPERT GUINNE3S would have gone to Parliament with a clear majority of the recorded votes behind him. The late Member for Haggeraton had no intention of again submitting himself for election. He therefore paid no attention to the register or to the Liberal organisation in the borough. Every- thing was permitted to drift, and as Sir RA.NDAL CREMER'S death came rather suddenly and quite unexpectedly there was no opportunity of recover-

ing lost ground. What the revelation of such a state of things emphasises is the fact that there are many other constituencies in which just the same process of drifting is going on. In Lord Mmiciumunv we recognise My. GEORGE WHITELEY, who was the predecessor of Mr. PEASE at the Whip Office. kird MARCHAMLEY represented the Pudsey Division of Yorkshire, and as Chief Whip he was the centre of Liberal organisation in the country; yet it turned out that Pudsey had been as much neglected as Haggerston afterwards proved to be, a fact which, if it had become known at an esrlier time, might have stood in the way of Mr. WHITELEY'S elevation to the House of Lords. It is wrong, we admit, to expect a sitting Member to find the means for keeping the party organisation thoroughly efficient. There are some Members who cannot do this because they have not the means at their disposal. It does, however, seem the clear duty of every Member either to do this work himself or to see that it is done by others. Neglect of the register is invariably fatal in the long run. It has only to be prolonged for year after year to ensure the I loss of the seat.

Mr. PEASE seems to us to be taking the proper course in regard to London. There is to be some overlooking of the constituencies from the head- quarters in Parliament-street. This is a policy which, we believe, might be extended to the whole country. We should not advocate any inter- ference in constituences where the party organisa- tion is being kept up as it should be. Members who are doing their duty in the matter, or who support local organisations that are really alive, might justly resent any intrusion from head- quarters; but undoubtedly the party Whips ought to know whether a constituency is organised or not, and, beyond this, ought to be prepared for action of some sort where there has been con- spicuous or dangerous neglect. For such a purpose they might enter into alliance with the National Liberal Federation, a body whose 'activities are not so apparent as, perhaps, they might be. If the Federation were to make it a part of its business to keep an oversight on party organisation throughout the whole country, supplying some stimulus where it was clearly needed, and securing help where that was neces- sary, it would be performing a service of the utmost value to Liberalism. There could, at any rate, be no more surprises like those of Pudsey and Haggerston. The condition of the party machine would at least be known in the proper quarter, and steps could then be taken to make it reasonably efficient. As matters stand, organisa- tion is almost certain to be neglected in all those cases where a Member does not intend again to ,stand for election, or where he is looking for promotion to "another place."

We are sure that very COUNCILLOR STOTT'S general regret will be felt in DEPA.RTURE. Warrington at the departure of Councillor Stott, J.P., to the far-distant colony of New Zealand. During the last fi.ve years, or more, he has been the lead- ing local representative of Labour and Socialism, and as such has attained considerable prominence It is as a Labour delegate to the Trades Council and the Town Council that Councillor S:ott has been best known, and in this capacity he has done a large amount of self-sacrificing work, the full value of which cannot be estimated. The interests of Labour as a policy and of the workers as a class, have been advanced to an uncommon degree by Councillor Stott's untiring efforts. The bulk of the progress has been made since he entered the Town Council, less than five years ago, and some of the advan- tages which he has secured for the workingmen of the town must inevitably be looked upon in the future as a birthright to be jealously guarded. Councillor Stott has always been conspicuous for level•headedness aud moderation, two qualities as invaluable as they are rare among agitators who spring from the masses to positions of trust and influence. We feel sure ke will find himself in congenial surroundings when once he gets to the advanced and rising colony of New Zealand. There is ample scope there for men of his type, and we can wish him nothing better than that he will find plenty of work to his hand, and the health and strength with which to carry it out.

Mr. John Maginn has Tim LITE MR. passed from our midst with Joxx Mkamr. startling suddenness. Few men were better known in Warrington, and a wide circle of friends will sadly miss the cheery, go-ahead Irishman who rose from the smallest beginnings to a position of authority and influence in our public life. "John Maginn," as he was usually termed in a familiar and friendly way, was not a man without faults, but he knew his failings and had the strength to avoid them. He was first and foremost and typically an Irishman—a man of large presence, loud voice,and big heart. What he set his hand to he strove with might and main to accomplish. Else he had remained a small man in a small circle. John Maginn resolved to go into the Town Council— and he got there by methods peculiarly his own. His famous "Kiddies' picnic " was open to question as an electioneering move, but it was a stroke of genius, and it was a grand thing for " the kiddies." Once in the Council, Mr. Maginn showed himself to be a fearless advocate of right against might. No man in Warrington ever espoused more vigorously the cause of the people against the brewers. He challenged some of the most cherished privileges of the latter, and had he been spared we have no doubt that he would have succeeded in a pet project of much importance, i.e.—the re-adjustment of public - house assessments in Warrington. So recently as the spring of this year Mr. Maginn threw himself with characteristic energy into the fight for the Licensing Bill, though oven then his health was in a precarious state and he was sacrificing, possibly, life itself. It was a source of regret to all who know him that John Maginn visibly weakened under the ravages of his complaint but none anticipated that the end would be so tragic. Though not a Warringtonian, the deceased gentle- man gave place to none in his love for the town of his adoption, and Warrington may,with justice, acclaim him one of the worthiest of her citizens.

IWe are all proud of the Tin: INFIRMARY. fact that Warrington now possesses an Infirmary thoroughly worthy cf the town, but an additional responsibility rests upon the townspeople to provide the funds necessary for the maintenance of the enlarged buildings. The Workingmen's Infirmary Committee have taken a commendable move in the matter by issuing an appeal to employees of the town and neighbourhood to increase the amount of their contributions. It is pointed out that the hospital is almost entirely dependent upon voluntary contributions. The necessary expendi- ture will, it is estimated, average £4,500 a year, whilst the assured income from invest- ments is only £B5O. There are 80 beds at present available for in-patients. During 1907 the in-patients treated numbered 547; out- patients, 3,390; home patients, 1,322; acci- dents and emergencies, 4,037; operations, 273. The Workingmen's Committee have done nobly in the past and hope to do still better in the future. The surest way of raising adequate funds would be to adopt a system of periodical contributions of a specified amount in all works. We believe this is already done in some establishments, but the method is far from general, and if all would adopt it the gain would be enormous and the Infirmary management would be relieved of considerable lanxiety as to ways and means.

We hardly think that Mr. Ma, W. P. RYLAND3 W. P. Rylands is to be con- ON IMPJILT3. gratnlated upon his latest excursion into the thorny path of the fiscal controversy. Speaking at Sankey he gave the Primrose Leaguers to understand that the German imports of manufactured articles are very much less than our own. 31a spoke of them as being worth only £55,000,000. Apparently, Mr. Rylands is unaware that the German classi- fication of manufactures is very different from our own, and that when onr Board of Trade classified our manufactured articles imported in the same way as German imports of the same kind, the latter came out at nearly £100,000,000 a year. As a matter of fact, the latest figures for imparts of manufactures by the three leading commercial nations are as follows: America is the largest importer of manufactured articles with £133,000,000 per annum ; the United Kingdom comes next with £128,000,000 per annum, and Germany a close third with £97,000,000 per annum. If then, high tariffs do not keep out manufactures, it would be interesting to kaow what Mr.Rylands thinks would be the effect on British imports of manufactures of the trumpery 10 per cent duty which Mr. Chamberlain proposed at Glasgow. ' Mr. Rylands also indulged in some reflections upon imports of raw materials, and was easily able to show that the 85,000,000 people of the United States and 60,000,000 people of Germany had , considerably increased their imports of raw materials in recent years. It is a fact which we decline to deplore. As for the British importa- tion of raw materials the record is an entirely satisfactory one. We notice that Mr. Rylands brings his record down to 1902. We submit the following table which measures the growth of British imports of raw materials by quantities in 20 years.

In the course, of a manly

MR. CAMERON letter to the "Times" ML:.. CORBETT M.P., AND Cameron CJThett, MP. for THE LICENSING BILL. the Tradeston Division of

Glasgow, severs his allegi- ance to the Conservative party, which he has represented in the House for 22 years. Mr. Corbett says: "I feel that I aria now in a false position in sitting among the opponents of a Government with whose temperance policy the brightest hopes of the future are bound up." Temperance reformers will readily admit that Mr. Corbett has done yeoman service to their cause throughout the fight for the Licensing Bill, but the letter he has just penned deals the shrewdest blow of all at the enemy. He adds "I find it difficult to listen with patience to the statement that it is an imposture to claim that the Government Bill is a temperance measure, knowing as I do that it is supported by all the organisations which exist for the promotion of temperance in this country." We would that a few more Unionist M.P.'s were half as candid. Too many of them oppose the Government 8,11 because it is a Government Bill and not because they doubt that it will lessen drinking and pro- mote temperance. Political honesty is a somewhat rare jewel, and consequently always hailed with delight when discovered. If Mr. Cameron Corbett forfeits the confidence of his constituents we shall be surprised. • Tney know a man when they see one in Scotland. Witness Dundee !

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

The gardens at the Beamont Council school are j ust now looking at their best and are evidence that a real love of the beautiful is being inculcated in the scholars, who, along with their teachers, are to be congratulated upon their success as gardeners. * * *

The number of local magistrates has this week been reduced by two,owing to the death of Mr. John Magi= and the departure of Councillor Stott. There are at least half-a-dozen candidates for honours. Perhaps the Lord Chancellor may now be induced to extend the list for Warrington. * * *

Councillor Stott, J.P. sails for New Zealand to-day. His friends are already saying that they expect him to emulate " Dick "Seddon of St. Helens and rise to the Premiership of the colony. Councillor Stott has promised to keep in touch with" Examiner" readers by occasional letters on social matters as he finds them in New Zealand. * *

In another column we publish a list of new works Which have been started in England since the passing of the new Patents Act. The foreigners have now to manufacture on the spot, and a further transference of capital and employment to this country is certain. The Warrington Towns Committee has been formed in the "nick of time," but so far it has not met. The members will not have to let the grass grow under their feet if they are to attract new industries to Warrington. * *

Like most great men, Sir John Brunner has a capacity for taking note of small things. In a letter to the " Westminster Gazette" he remarks :—" I was lately told by Professor Sir Robert Boyce, F.R.S., of the Liverpool University, that there would be one- fiftieth of the usual number of flies in Liverpool this year, as a result of the action of the Committee, guided by Mr. Newstead's report. Mr. Newstead I hope, soon to be appointed Professor of Economic Entomology. Verily, the old country is waking up !"

===== 0002601_19080822_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
** *

Another individual of less renown has _also shown an uncommon interest in the house-fly. In this case the popular pest was made use of. The facts wore related at Belfast on Tuesday, when a Iv oman accused her husband of meannesss stating that he actually put a fly in the sugar, and covered up the bowl so as to detect her if she took a spoonful out. We should imagine that the sweetness of this poor woman's life was a trifle fly-blown. * *

Supporters of the Licensing Bill will profit by a close study of the remarkable proceedings at the annual meeting of Messrs. Guinness and Co. on Saturday. The profits shown were £1,242,000, and a dividend of 27 per cent. was paid for the year. Moreover, it was proposed to absorb the reserves accumulated from past years by issuing new stock to the ordinary shareholders in the proportion of one share for every abate held. That is a bonus of 100 per cent. In Dublin, at any rate, it will be difficult to make an appeal for pity for the sorrows of the poor brewer. * * *

- The death of Mr. T. R. Shepherd removed a figure from the Warrington streets which has been familiar for generations. It is something like 30 years since Mr. Shepherd retired from his profession as a solicitor and scores of people knew him by sight who had no idea who he was. The aged, bent, old-world figure of the deceased gentleman was a familiar sight in Bewsey-street. He used to walk quietly along with his hands clasped in front of him in an attitude of meditation or prayer. He might easily have passed for a Dickens character and certainly seemed to belong to another century.

One cannot help wondering what our ancestors of a century ago, if they could revisit the earth, would think of the leviathan ships which the White Star Company proposes to build next year, with their 860 ft. of length and their tonnage of 45,000-50,000. When, in the year 1800, the French built a vessel " of most extraordinari'size," 172 ft. long, and of 2,850 tons, she was pronounced to bs "entirely unfit for service, arid hath never been out of harbour." " And the Spaniards are said," to quote a contemporary writer, " to have built one still larger • but on sending this unwieldy monster to sea, they 'found that she must have been lost if they had not had the precaution to send out two other ships with her, which towel and brought her back again." Less than seventy years ago the first Cunarrs, 207 ft. long and of 1,131 tons burden, were considered marvels of size, although all four of them could have been placed in a line on the ' deck of one of these White Star liners, which will have ten times their aggregate tonnage.

The death of Mr. Ira i 5. Sankey will be genuinely mourned by many hundreds of thousands of persons, even though in these days a new generation has grown up since the last of the campaigns of Messrs. Moody and Sankey. It is a little difficult now, perhaps, to realise the vast power which these evangelists showed on their different visits, or the source of their success. The truth is that the combination of the two men was uniquely equipped for success, each supplementing the other. As a singer and composer Mr. Sankey was largely self-trained, but he had a beautiful voice and a real ear for a tune that would be popular. Some of his hymns and a great deal of his music are being sung in the Christian churches all over the world. Had he chosen he might have made a great personal fortune, but the profits of his books have gone to aid the work in which he was interested. For five years now Mr. Sankey had been removed by blindness from active work, but the great religious movement which he helped to create and inspire has gone forward, and in many languages to-day hymns are being sung to the tunes to which he wedded them for all time. * * * We were glad to sea from his article in the "Labour Leader " that Mr. Keir Hardie does not follow Messrs. Blatchforcl and Hyndman in yielding to the German panic. We believe he is right in saying that the attitude of these two gentlemen wotld be "repudiated with practical unanimity by the Socialist and trade union movement if it could be put to the vote." The "Clarion," by the way, itself provided an admirable antidote to its own poison in a letter from Admiral Sir Cyprian Bridge, which appeared in last Friday's issue. The main point is that, even if we impute the worst possible motives and intentions to Germany, the mechanical difficulty of attacking this country, while it holds the command of the sea, is such that we could afford to be calm about it. And conversely, we may say to Germans who—incredible as it may seem—are persuaded that we mean to attack them, that such an operation would be a desperate hazard, which no statesmen in their senses would undertake.

Some interesting remarks on the Derby millions have been made by the "Morning Leader," as follows :—The late Lord Derby has left an estate of nearly 4,000,000. By profession he was " a land- owner," no less than 70,000 acres of English soil being subject to the autocratic control of this single member of the House of Peers. His estates were chiefly situated in Lancashire, and the enormous industrial development in that county during the past century has without any exertion on the part of the , Stanleys doubled their fortune over and over again. This vast wealth consisted almost entirely of unearned increment, and it is a significant fact that the will does not contain any bequests of a public nature. The death duties, fortunate:y, will reclaim for the people a portion of their own, and in the development of the principle of these duties in association with the taxa- tion of land values we may look forward to a more equitable adjustment of the balance in the future. i An attempt is likely to be made in the autumn to - get a Bill passed through Parliament for the relief ' of the teacher who from no fault of his own is deprived _ of his post through the closing of the school. The - contention on his behalf is that compensation should be paid by the Government for his loss of office, and an adequate pension guaranteed him, as by depriving him of his means of living, he is losing an opportunity to increase his pension by further payment during recorded service. At present no sustentation le provided by the National Union of Teachers. A few teachers might obtain a small disablement allow- ance, should they reach the age of sixty-five, and others might receive a very small pension. The newly-formed National Council of Church Teachers are prepared to act on behalf of these teachers, and probably other organisations interested in the welfare of women will render help. The matter is regarded as pressing by the promoters, because they consider the complaint would be very greatly aggravated in the doubtful event of the Education Bill of 1003, , with its contracting-out scheme, becoming law.

DEATH OF MR. H. N. HOUGHTON.

Warrington has lost another respected citizen by the death of Mr. Hamlet Nowall Houghton, iron- monger, 33, Bridge-street, who passed away at his residence, 9, Whitefield-road, Stockton Heath, at 19 o'clock on Wednesday morning, after a long and painful illness. The deceased gentleman, who was fifty-six years of age, had been confined to his bed for about ten months. His many friends heard of his illness with sincere regret, for his kindly and unassuming disposition had gained for him a warm place in their esteem. He had carried on business as an ironmonger at Market Gate for many years, and when his shop was required for street improvement purposes he removed to the premises in Bridge street. He never took a prominent part in public affairs but was best known as a devoted Churchman. He was particularly attached to St. James' Church, Latchford, where his willing help will be greatly missed. He was a lay representative up to the time of his death, and some years ago he fulfilled the duties of Vicar's warden. Ha was also a foundation manager of St. James' School. He was a staunch member of the Warrington Branch of the English Church Union and also served on the committee of the Warrington Church Society. Great sympathy is felt with the widow; in her bereavement. The funeral will take place at St. Thomas's Church, Stockton Heath, at 1.30 this (Saturday) afternoon.
