WATER POLO.

WARRINGTON v. SOUTH HILL.

These teams met in the Warrington Corporation Baths on Thursday evening. Warrington defended tha deep end in the first half. Sutton swam away splendidly' but was fouled near goal. From a foul HaresnaPe received and opened the scoring for South Hill. After some even play South Hill again attacked, and White i scored a second. Immediately afterwards White aided ; a third. Shelvey scored the fourth with a beautiful long shot. Half-time : South Hill 4 goals, Warring ton nil. Warrington showed up much better in this , half, and Allen scored an easy goal. W Ashton, who A swam the length of the bath, scored a second. White t scored a fifth goal for South Hill, and Turner scored a third for Warrington. South Hill showed better combination, and won comfortably by 5 goals to 3.—The final for the fifty yards members' race resulted as follows :-1, J. Sutton ; 2, E. Miller ; 3, J Savage.

Insurance Dispute at War rington. A case arising out of a disputed insurance policl was heard at the Warrington Police Court yesterday' The parties concerned were Mary Hankinson, Chester street and William Jones, Bramhall-street, who claimed £lO 4s. 6d. insurance money on the policy of Herbert Hankinson, in the Albion Insurance Company. gr. Browne on behalf of Jones, stated that the deceased person, Herbert Hankinson, was a brother-in-law of his client. Some time ago Mrs. Hankinson, the mother, insured the deemed in the Albion Friendll Society for £lO. She could not afford to keep tER the premium, and in consequence she asksa the collector (Kr. Hough) to take it to gr; William Jones, her son in-law. It was then agrefr! to take over the policy, and the premium was palo for three years. Nothing was heard until her sall died on June 2, when she claimed the whole of the money, refusing to accept £4 10s.for funeral expenses. —Mr. Steel said Mrs. Hankinson had paid the premium for eleven years ; and was willing to Pi the amount that Jones, her son-in-law, had paid.' The magistrates decided in favour of Mrs. Hankinsoe• Mr. Jones was awarded one-third of the remainder . after funeral expenses had been paid.

Jones announced that George Dodd, caretaker of the Smallpox Hospital, Moore, died on July 30th, and he had made temporary arrangements with the late caretaker's son and daughter to continue until suitable arrangements were made. ITIIE COST OF PAUPERISM. k The Clerk reported that the Local Government Board 'ad forwarded the half-yearly return on the cost of ' Pauperism. Taking the unions and parishes in London, the rate per head of the population was 2s. 11Id. ; for the whole country (London and the provinces) the rate was is. 10d. per head ; for the Unions outside . London the rate was Is. bld. per head ; for the county ' lii! Chester it averaged ls. Qd., and in the Runcorn nion it was is. 33d., so that Runcorn compared very favourably.

COMPLAINT ABOUT TIIE HOSPITAL. CHARGES NOT SUBSTANTIATED.

VAGRANCY QUESTION, „The Clerk stated that the Inter-County Committee on agrancy recommended the adoption of a system of WaY tickets for casual paupers and, in order that there Plight be no necessity for begging, the establishment of , erect(' stations for the supply of mid-day meals of half- Pound of bread and two ounces of cheese, the cost to oe borne from a fund provided by the Lancashire, , Cheshire, Cumberland and Westmoreland Unions; and ill certain cases the provision of a mid-day meal to ''agrants on their discharge from a Workhouse. The co:operation of the police authorities was asked for.

lAt a special meeting OI the Isolation Hospital Com- mittee Mr. Higginson, Halton, and Mrs. Littler, Frodsham, attended and made complaints with regard to the hospital administration. Mrs. Higginson, wife of one of the complainants, was also interviewed. The committee afterwards heard the medical superintendent, matron and charge nurse (Miss Bisset) with reference to the allegations which had been made. The com- naittee resolved (1) " That having heard the allegations blade by Mr. Higginson and Mrs. Littler with regard to the treatment of the two children who died in the hospital, this committee is satisfied that the staff of the hospital is exonerated from any blame in the matter. (2) That the Committee consider I it is imperative that Dr. Bower should he in telephonic I communication with the hospital in order that prompt intimation can be conveyed to him in critical cases." The clerk read a letter from Mr. Higginson stating that if the District Council did not take prompt steps to remedy the abuses prevailing in the institution he would take proceedings against the Council for the loss of his child. Mr. Woodward said the Committee went very care- fully into the charges and were unanimously of opinion that they were not substantiated. With reference to the letter from Mr. Higginson, they well understood that parents who had lost their children in that way felt it most keenly and the Con mitteesympathised with them. They had, however, no sympathy with the diu position to lay the loss to the charge of those who the Committee were quite sure had done their best for the patients. As far as he knew this was the only case of complaint out of some 135 treated. In almost every case the highest tributes of thanks and praise had been awarded to the nurses and staff. _ .

Mi.. Wright, who iepresented the Board at a Confer- • on the question,said the Board could not do better than join in the scheme. The amount they would have to pay would be very small. The Chairman said that the scheme would tend to atop begging. Mr. Mynekyme : What will it cost in salaries to give the food ? The Clerk said the arrangement would probably be that food would be supplied at certain shops on tickets being presented. It would not be a heavy matter. Mr. Wright said he did not think they would require Sty additional officials. it was agreed to join in the scheme. CO VNTY EXPENDITURE. IMPERIAL GRANTS WANTED. It was necesFary that the house should be in keeping with the other buildings. The recommendation was approved.

HATTON SHOW.

VILLAGERS' CREDITABLE DISPLAY.

The residents of the charming; old-world village of Hatton pride themselves not a little on their horticul- ture, and not without good cause, for at present the gardens ia the locality represent perfect pictures of colour and beauty. Of late years Hatton has become quite famed for its show, and Thursday witnessed the holding of the fourteenth exhibition promoted by the Horticultural Society. The place presented a scene of unwonted animation, visitors flocking into it from Warrington and district, and all were highly pleased with what they saw. The show was a great credit to Fall the villagers, and the choice exhibits, tastefully arranged, excited general admiration. Mr. James Parr had again placed a field adjoining Hatton Hall at the disposal of the society, and a better spot for show purposes could not have been Lund. While there was a slight falling off in the entries, the 'umber of competitors was actually higher, and the quality of the exhibits was well up to the high standard associated with Hatton Show. The table decorations were a special feature the competitors displaying much artistic taste in the arrangement of beautiful flowers. Lady Greenall offered special prizes for the prettiest dinner table (only outside the fi

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Cricket.

The West Lancashire League reaches its flaal stage to-day when Earlestown and St. Helens Recs. will play off the deciding encounter at Newton. Earlestown had an excellent chance of being champions last Saturday, but they were badly beaten at home by Prescot, and consequently only tied with the Recs., each team having 15 points from 12 games. Mr. James Dodds, the President of the League, will attend the match to-day and present the medals to the victorious team.

Warrington have long since said goad-bye to the championship, but they wound up the League season on Saturday with a substantial victory over Stanley, the wooden-spoonists. The match was played under the best conditions, and Warrington going first to the wicket put on 185 runs fpr the loss of half their wickets. The popular skipper, B. Silcock, was in a lively mood and rattled up a fine 62 not out in confident style. T. Wright and the earlier batsmen failed to do much, bat excellent support was given to the Captain by S. Partridge, a promising young batsman who hit up 47, and W. H. Podmore, one of the veterans who has had a good season. He made 27 and got a couple of valuable wickets. Cresswell and Callon each had four victims, the visitors not giving a great deal of trouble in compiling a lifeless 80.

The second eleven spoiled their good record by taking a weak team to Stanley and succumbing by 161 to 119. The \vlis and S. Btxter gave the side a good start with scores of 20 and 4), but only Houghton and Plinston of the succeeding batsmen did anything at all. Both reached the twenties. Most of the others got "ducks."

Only one match was played in the Warrington and District League, Walker's recording a narrow victory at the expense of the " Guardian ' team. A. Ross's 21, out of 31, was the crowning effort. In the Junior League the biggest perf3rmance was put up by Mirshhouse-lane who smothered St. Mary's by 104 to H. Carman took live wickets for six runs, while W. Yates and T. Warburton did most of the scoring with 27 and 33 respectively. 1 For St. JRIII.39'S Juniors, L. Walker lei off with 33 against Holy Trinity, and S. Leigh did the coveted "hat trbk " at the expense of Warrington Excelsiors.

Sankey Wire Mills did well at High Legh, replying to the villagers 47 with 53. Woodcock subscribed 23 late on in the innings or the result might have beea reversed.

At Arley the second team from Grappenhall had to submit to defeat. Dr. Love scored 22 out of 63 for the home team. while Grappenhall could only raise 42. J. Mottram was the only man to reach double figures, and be equalled " Mr. Extras " with a modest ten. Slack did some good bowling, but "love " IT3S too strong for him. _ .

A E Burdett, Castle Rubber ; R Dandy, Robertson's ; ' S Pair, Robinson's; J Appleton, Monk's ; A Gleave, I Whitecross ; W Ellison,Crosfleld's ; R Jefferson,Monk's; A Scott, Longford ; T Harrison, Houghton's ; J Melborne, Orford ; J Welsby,Greeuings ; J Broadhurt, I Fletchers ' • reserves, J Gleave, Whitecross; Joe Locker, Ropery ; C Perry, Ropery ; P Patrick, Greening's. Referee, J Willett ; measurer, J Broadhurst. At 2.2) this (S aturday) afternoon Lady Greenall will , formally present the Greenall Challenge Cap to the I Association.

The final for the -Crosfield Challenge Cup presented for competition by members of the Liberal Club was played on Saturday in the presence of a large gathering. W. robin and H. Daintith were the finalists, and the result was a narrow victory for Tobin. At the conclu- sion of the match Councillor Wilkinson presented the cup to the winner. Amongst others present were Councillor Locker, Mr. J. Upson and Mr. J. Howes, Jun. The victory entitles the cup holder to a gold medal and there is also a medal for the runner-up. In handing the cup to Mr. Tobin, Councillor Wilkinson congratulated him on the great honour of being the first man to have his name inscribed on the cup and expressed the hope that he would long be spared to play in future contests. Mr. robin suitably replied and said he hoped that next year the cup would be won by his opponent, Mr. Daintith. The victor's health was then drunk from the cup.

-- The final in the handicap promoted by the Prince of Wales Lodge of Oddfellon-s was played on Saturday and VV. Roughley by defeating W. Cottam becomes the holder of the handsome silver cup presented by Mr. A. H. Crosfleld, M.P., for twelve months. He is cordially to be congratulated upon his victory.

At a meeting of the Constitution Lodge Independent Order of Oddfellows (Manchester Unity), held at the Pelican Hotel, Buttermarket-street, on Saturday even- ing list, N.G. ‘Vdson presiding, and V.G.Ellison in the bottom chair, it was decided to hold a bowling handicap in connection with the Lodge. It was announced that P.P.G.M. Thomas S. Steel had promised to present a silver challenge cup for competition amongst the mem- bers of the Lodge.—Oa the proposition of P.G. Burdett, seconded by P.G. Henry Taylor, a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to P.G.N.I. Thomas S. Steel for his generous oiler. The following officers and members were appointed a committee to make arrangements for the main George H. W. Steel, N.G. Wilson, V.G. Ellison, E.S. Mellor, I.T. Grounds, P.C. Henry Taylor, P.G. Burdett, P.G. Holloway, Secretary Taylor, P.G. Watson and Bro. George Holbrook. It is expected that the committee will arrange for the cup and other valuable prizes to be competed for on either the 12th or lath of September.

===== 0002601_19080822_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
Bowling.

The great county match has been decided and Bull's Head are to be 'acclaimed champions of Lancashire and Cheshire. l'hey well deserve their victory and probably no one will congratulate them more heartily than the members of the Cemetery team who had to try conclu- sions with them in the final. It speaks well for War- rington's standing in the bowling world that it was able to furnish both the finalists in such an important competition. During the match excitement was at fever heat, the position at times being exceedingly close, but in the end Bull's Head triumphed by 2l points. It is nine years since the championship came to Warrington, and on that occasion the Warrington South team met and defeated Wigan by three points. The losers have appeared five times in the final since ISM', when they won the championship, that year beating Radcliffe. Last year Bull's Head met Pendleton Belmont in the final but had to admit defeat.

Saturday's match attracted a great gathering, which inoluded all the leading officials of the association. Tom Ellison, the L. and C. amateur champion, wait drawn agaigast his cousin, J. Cleave, one of the youngest members of the Bull's Head team. Cleave won the jack and placed his first wood inches from the little one, only to see Ellison beat it, the amateur champion receiviag well-merited applause all round the green. Cleave played his second wood, again inches from the jack, whilst Ellison rested on his other wood,but only counted one. The closeness of the bowling will be judged when an ordinary sized " footer " would have covered the whole set. Ellison took the young player on a2l yards mark, smothering the jack each end, and the spectators early on witnessed some of the finest bowling over seen on the Crmston grzen.

At the sixth end he led S-0. Visions of the heavy defeat of young Gleave began to dawn on the followers of the Bull's Head team, when at the twelfth end Ellison was leading 14-3. A couple at the next end gave Gleave an opening which he made good use of. Right from corner to corner he gradually overhauled the champion, and in twelve ends he made the gams 13-10. Ellison got a couple, the game being IS all. At the 30th end Gleave wanted one to Ellison two, the amateur champion winning at the last end 21-20.

Hew keenly the games were fought can be judged when at the end of the first four games five points divided the teams. A fine performance by the Cemetery captain (G. Ball, jun.), gave his side the lead. Swanton, his opponent, stuck to him like a leech, peeling at 2-2,4-4, 6-6, B—S, —9, and 12-12. From this point, however, Ball added three 2's and five l's, winning by 21-14. Bolton made a welcome re-appear- ance for the Warrington team, his 21-16 fully justify- ing his selection in so impartant a match. At the end of the eighth game only nine points divided the teams, the spectators anticipating a close struggle until Charlie Roberts, playing for Bull's Head, proved him- self the hero of the match, his fifteen points victory over T. Harrison placing his side out of danger.

Mr. Coombs, on presenting the Crostleld Cup to Albert Gleave, the popular Bull's Head captain, con- gratulated both teams on reaching the final stage of the L. and C. club championship competition.

The Warrington Works Association's annual main commenced on SAturday. There was a fine entry and great enthusiasm was shown in the contests. The scores wilt be found in another column.

To-day 'the charity match with Eccles Workshops Association will take place at Bank Quay and as the proceeds will go to such deserving institutions as the Infirmary and Dispensary and Nursing Association there should ben large gate. We hope local trundlers will maintain their reputation for assisting charities by assembling in full force. The match promises to be most interesting.

The following is the Warrington team :—G Ball (captain). Rugby Football.

Practice matches are now in full swing. On Saturday last a match between eight of the let Team, and 12 of the " A" team took place, and resulted in a win for the Ist Team by 23 points to nil. A practice was also held on Tuesday evening.

To-day (Saturdiy) another practice will take place; kick-off G. 30. It is expected that a full team will turn out, including the Welshmen. Admission to the practice matches, Id. The gross takings are for the Infirmary.

Cycling.

The members of the Warrington Club journeyed to Hunts Cross on Saturday to measure their skill on the bowling green, with a tern from the Commsrcial Travellers' Association. From various causes the turn-up was rather small, still a very pleasant series of games was played, the cyclists ultimately winning both the singles and doubles by a small majority, -- Tuesday evening found a section of the club at Bollington again, either the beauties of this spot or host Jefferson's genital smile seeming to have quite a magnetic influence on the members.

Wednesday evenings at Sankey are plainly indicating the approach of Autumn, the time for bowling being now very limited.

To-day the annual flitting match takes place at Little Legh, this fanction generally being conspicuous by the s:nall amount of fish, but tke large amount of fun, obtained.

===== 0002601_19080822_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
Water Polo.

Warrington visited Leigh on ruesday evening last in a friendly match with the Leigh Polo team. It was not a very interesting game, the bath being unsuitable for Water Polo. Warrington scored first, but could not combine at all and in the end were defeated by goals to 3. C. Ashton, who is playing for Hyde Seal this season, assisted Leigh.

The League match with South llill was plsyed at the BAths, Legh-street, before a fiir audion^m. The game is described in another column and the homesters lost 5-3.

Some very exciting swimming was seen in the 50 yards members' handicap. There were 30 entries, which shows that interest is being taken in swimming in the club. There was also an exhibition of back stroke swimming by the Captain of the South Hill team, Mr. Haresnape, who was third in the World's Championship, at the Olympic Sports. Master Miller also gave a dis- play of floating in the water. Considering that he has only been a member of the Club this year his display was very floe. These two events were very much enjoyed by the spectators. The Works Squadron Com- petition, which should have been drawn for on Tuesday last, will be drawn on Monday evening. Warrington play Atherton away on Tuesday evening.

LEAD POISONING.

Warrington Workman's Compensation Award Terminated.

At the \Carrington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Shiress Will, K.C., Mr. Lindou Riley, barrister (instructed by Messrs. Sharpe and Dtvison, Chester) applied on behalf of the Mersey White Lead Co., Limited, for the termination of an award under the Workmen's Compensation Act in respect of lead poisoning sustained by Thomas Kivelle, 72, Chorley-street.—The respondent was not legally represented. Mr. Riley said the award was made by his Honour Deputy Judge Maxwell on January 23rd. There was an array of medical testimony pro and con as to whether lead poisoning as a fact had ever taken place. It was fair to the applicant and to those who represented him to ,say that it was presented as a mild case and the leading doctor on his side, who spoke to an examination in the previous November, said that his opinion at that time was that the man would be fit for work in two or three weeks. At all events, the deputy- judge regarded it as established that Kivelle had to some extent suffered from lead poisoning and an award of 14s. per week was made. Instead of giving him the short time that was suggested at the beginning as sufficient, Kivelle had now had something like nine months' compensation money and he (Mr. Riley) would call three doetors, one of whom appeared before and two of whom approached the case without any pre- possessions in the matter, and they all agreed in saying there was not the slightest trace of lead poisoning or any of its effects at the present time. Dr. Fox said that since the date of the award he had examined Kivelle forty or fifty times, On the 23rd April he examined him in company with Dr. Joseph and he had then entirely recovered from the effects of lead poisoning. Botween March 22nd and June 13th Kivelle gained 101bs in weight. Dr. Joseph said that when he examined Kivelle on April 23rd be had none of the symptoms of lead poisoning. He believed the man would be fit to work as a labourer.

Dr. Langdale gave similar evidence as than examina- tion on June 18th and said he considered Kivelle was tit to resume his ordinary employment. Kivelle stated that when the doctors told him he was fit to work he went to the White Lead Works and asked for employment, but was refused. He had also tried at other places unsuccessfully. Dy Mr. Riley : Before he brought his claim, he had been dismissed from the works. His Honour said the evidence was all one way and he had no alternative but to find in favour of the appli- cants. The evidence was that for some time the respondent had been fully recovered from the effects of lead poisoning and the doctors said he was as fit as ever he was for his old work. If that were so, he (the Judge) had no alternative but to fled against him. He, however, proposed to reduce the compensation to the nominal sum of one penny, so as not to put him out of Court altogether but to keep the claim alive, and then if respondent was able to satisfy him that he was still suffering from lead poisoning he might he able to do justice in the matter. Mr. Riley contended that applicants ought to be relieved from the obligation to keep the matter open, in view of the medical evidence on Kivelle's behalf that ha should be able to resume work in about three weeks.

His Honour said that under those circumstances he would put an end to the award.

===== 0002601_19080822_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
TERRIBLE COLLIERY EXPLOSION.

ONE OF PEARSON AND KNOWLES' PITS WRECRED. Seventy-Six ITves Lost.

A colliery disaster, which has already proved to be the most serious that has occurred in the Wigan district for many years, took place on Tuesday evening in the No. I pit of the Maypole Collieries, Abram, about four miles from Wigan, belonging to the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd., having been takers over by them about IS months ago from the Moss Hall Colliery Co. There were over 70 men down the mine when the explosion took place,and nearly all their lives have been lost.

Shortly after five o'clock a terrific explosion occurred, which caused a shock to be felt for a distance of two miles around, followed by clouds of dust and smoke which rose to a great height above the head gear at the mouth of the shaft, while debris was thrown in all directions. Several men who were engaged at one side of the pit had a narrow escape, as a large piece of wood was thrown amongst them, just missing a man named Charles Bolton, of Warrington. Though the ventilation plant was blown, away, rescue operations of the most heroic character were at once in operation. About half a mile from the scene of disaster is the Wigan Junction Colliery, which communicates with the Maypole pit, and down this a rescue party descended, returning at ten o'clock with three men— named William Dolan, Smith and Draper—having had to travel 800 yards to get at them. They found the air breathable at that distance, but beyond it was stated to be gaseous, and several pit ponies were found dead, while five of the rescue party were affected by gas. Large crowds of people from all directions quickly gathered around the scene of the disaster, among thera being managers of other collieries, who eagerly volun- teered their services in any direction in which they might be utilized. Amongst those assembled around the pit were numerous relatives of the entombed men, and many pathetic scenes were witnessed among them. several of the directors of the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd., were soon at the scene of the accident, including Messrs. Harmood Banner, ALP., W. Bleckly, and W. P. Rylands. The pit almost immediately took fire and rendered rescue operations practically hopeless. The officials were soon compelled to admit the sad fact that the poor fellows down the mine must all be dead. Latest returns put the number of the missing at 7G. The fire is still raging, and rescue work was again interrupted. It has been decided to flood the pit. This will, it is estimated, take a month or six weeks ; and until then the remaining bodies must remain below. Heroic rescue work on the part of two men— Thomas Morris and Fox—who went down to the assistance of a number of pumpers belonging to the rescue party who could not get out has been recom- mended for recognition for the King's medal. Putting pocket handkerchiefs over their mouths, the two men managed to reach the pumping house and get the men out in a state of complete exhaustion. During all the weeks that the mine is inactive the 500 employees will be idle, so that a considerable loss of wages will be experienced in the locality, whilst there will be a heavy drain on the funds of the Lulea- shire and Cheshire Miners' Federation. There is much speculation ss to the cause of the explosion, but on this point no trustworthy information is obtainable. It is computed that the compensation payable to the relatives of the victims will amount to .£lO,OOO or £ll,OOO.

'The Warrington workmen of the Pearson and Knowles Co. Ltd. at the atllam and Bewsey Forge, mill and engineering departments, have promised the sum of Z5O as a donation to the fund for the relief of the bereaved

The seven bodies brought up from the pit and identified by relatives were viewed by the Coroner and jury on Thursday night, qnd subsequently in a little schoolroom, some distance from the pit, the inquest was opened.

. - After formal evidence had been called the inquest was adjourned until September 29th. The 69 bodies yet in the pit will have to be left there until the flooding of the mine has been completed and the water pumped out again.

THE KING AND ABRAM DISASTER. A message has been received by the Mayor of Wigan from King Edward at Marienbad. The King expresses himself much grieved to hear of the terrible disaster which has occurred at the Maypole Colliery, and wishes to be kept informed of the earliest intelligence of those whose fate is still doubtful. His Majesty deplores the loss of life and sincerely sympathises with those bereaved.

The Bray Seal In the Museum. The grey seal recently caught at Paddington Lock is now on exhibition in the Zoology Room in the Museum. The animal has been stuffed and mounted by Messrs. George and Charles J. Mounfield, of Buttermarket-street. The following note on the capture was published in the " Zoologist " for July: —" Occurrence of the Grey Seal (Halichoerus grypus) in the Mersey.—An adult male grey seal *vas shot in Paddington Lock, on the Woolston new cut of the River Mersey, above Warrington Bridge. It was driven into the lock and killed on June 17th, 1908. For two or three days before it was killed two seals had been noticed between Atherton' s Quay and Warrington Blidge, and several unsuc. cessful attempts were made to shoot them. in order to reach the cut the seal must have ascended Howley Weir Latchford, which it probably did at high tide. The animal, which I examined in the flesh, measured :—From nose to tip of tail, 7ft. Gin.; from nose to longest toe of hind foot, Bft. ; girth of body posterior to fore flippers, 4ft. Bin. ; length of head, 141 n. Mr. T. A. Coward has seen the skin and skull and confirms the identification ; he has seen ths grey seal off the coasts of Lleyn and Anglesey, where it has occirred on several occasions, and is of opinion that the species may breed on the North Wales Coast. This is the second recorded instance of the occurrence of the grey seal in the Mersey, the previous one being in the winter of 1860-61, when one was captured in the Canada Dock, Liverpool.

Lymm Coachman Commits Suicide. Frank Brooker, a coachman, living with his parents in the Higher-lane, Lymm, was found dead in an out- house, by his mother, soon after she got up on Tues- day morning. He was at his work on the preceding day and went to bed as usual, but must have got up early and cut his throat without anyone hearing anyl thing unusual. His father was for many years butler to Mr. Dewhnrst, of Oughtrington Hall, and his brothers are all stencil-, hardworking men Deceased worked in Lymm all his life—for some time recently for Miss Fox, of Brookfield, who speaks highly of his general conduct.

At the Borough Police Court lefore the Mayor and other magistrates, Sidney Warburton (13) resid- ing at 25, Lord Nelson-street ; Ernest Pownell (10), 29, Lord Nelson-street and William Perzy (11), 23, Lord Nelson-street, were charged with throwing stones and with breaking two panes of glass in the tannery in Vernon-street. Defendants pleaded guilty. Detective Clough proved the case, and said that he had received numerous complaints of windows being broken in that neighbourhood. The defendants after being severely repremanded by the Chairman were fined is. each.

FOSTAL AND SAVNGS BANK REGULATIONS

For further particulars see "Post Office Guide," at any Post o;fiee. POSTAGE STAMPS.. _

Books containing 12 penny and 23 halfpenny stamps are now on sale at each post oflice. Price 2s.

INLAND CORRESPONDENCE.

The term-" Inland Correspondence" means cor- respondence posted within the United Kingdom, including the Isle of Man end the Channel Islands. "Inland Correspondence " is divided into the following classes : (a) Letters (d) Newspapers (b) Postcards (e) Parcels (c) Halfpenny packets Not only letters, but any document or article (with a few trifling exceptions) which can he trans- mitted by post at all, may be sent by letter post, the only limitation being that of size. LETTER POST. The prepaid rate of postage is as follows Not exceeding 4 ozs. in weight ... ld. For every additional 2 07. d . A letter posted unpaid is chargeable on delivery with double postage; a letter insufaciently paid, with double the deficiency. No letter may exceed two feet in length, one foot in width, or one foot in depth.

POST-CARDS. The prepaid rate of postage ou every post-card is one half-penny, and on every reply post-card one penny. A post-card posted unpaid is chargeable on delivery with one penny. Cards used as post-cards must be made of ordinary card-board, not thicker than the materi.:l used for the thickest official post-card. They may not exceed 5} inches in length by 3} incl4es in width, or be less than 31 inches iu length by 21 inches in width. A gummed label (not to exceed 2 inches long and finch wide) bearing the address may now be atfixed to the address side of card. If ary of the rules relating to a post-card be infringed, the card will be treated as a letter. HALFPENNY PACICET POST. The Halfpenny Packet Post can be used only for packets not exceeding 2oz. in weight. Printed matter, Brc., not in the nature of a letter, may be transmitted by this method. NEWSPAPER POST. The prepaid rate of postage on a registered news- paper, including any supplement thereto, whether posted singly or with others is Id. Newspapers not registered at the General Post Olice are treated in the pcst like any other printed matter.

PARCEL POST. The rates of postage on parcels areas follows: For a parcel ... ... not exceeding 1 lb. 0 3 jp exceeding 1 lb. f 1 2 lb. 0 4 ~ „ 2 lb. 39 3 lb. 0 , 5 ti „ 3 lb. I, 5 lb. 0 6 , I • ff 5 lb. ~ 7 lb. 0 7 11 ii 7 lb. ,i 8 lb. 0 8 ,i „ 8 lb. fl 9 lb. 0 9 ii „ 9 lb. ii ' 10 lb. 010 „ 10 lb. 11 lb. 011 i, No parcel may exceed 11 lb. in weight. The size allowed for an inland parcel is : Greatestlength... ... ... ... 3 ft. 6 in. Greatest length and girth combined 6 ft. 0 in. For examPle—A parcel measuring 3ft. Gin, in its longest dimensions as much as 2ft. 6in. round its thickest part ; a short parcel may be thicker ' • thus, if it measure no more than 3ft. in length, it may measure as much as 3ft. round its thickest part. The postatic on a parcel must be pa i€l. Mark each parcel Parcel Post." A parcel must not be posted in a letter box, but presented at the counter of a post office, and the sender should see that the weight, size and postage are in order before leaving.

PARCELS .(FOREIGN AND COLONIAL). - Parcels are accepted for parcel post to most foreign countries under conditions similar to the inland regulations, except that for customs require- ments the contents must be declared. The customs charges can be prepaid or collected on delivery. The postage varies to different countries and must be prepaid. MONEY ORDERS (INLAND.) The postal authorities cannot issue a single money order for moire than £4O, and will only cash them at the money order office at which they are made payable, unless they are crossed, when they can be paid through a bank. No fractional part of a id. can be contained in a money order. The rates of poundage are :—Not exceeding £l, 2d. ; £3, 3d.; £lO, ; £2O, 6 ; £3O, Bd. ; £4O, 10,1. By paying. an additional 21. and cost of the telegram of advice, a money order can be telegraphed under certain restrictions. MONEY ORDERS (FOREIGN AND COLONIAL.) The maximum amount for which a money order may be issued for places abroad varies in each particular place. The poundage rates are :—Not exceeding £l,3d. ; £2, Gd. ; and above that, 31. for each additional .22 or portion of it. A fee of 6d. in addition to the ordinary poundage, together with cost of telegram of advice, enables money orders to be telegraphed to certain places abroad. POSTAL ORDERS.

These are obtainable at any money order &Noe in the United Kingdom, and possess the advantage over money orders that they can be cashed at any money order office. If crossed, they can be paid into a bank. They are issued for fixed amounts, rising by 6d., from 6d. to £1 Is. The poundage fees are :—id. up to 2s. Gd.; Id. up to 153.; fil. up to 21s. Before parting with a postal order, always fill in the name of the parson to whom the amount is to be paid. Each order is numbered, as is also the counterfoil attached, which latter should he retained and name of payee filled in. The Post- master-General will entertain no further claim after an order is once paid. Stamps to the amount of sd. can be affixed to make up odd sums.

TELEGRAMS (INLAND). The Postmaster-General possesses,almost entirely, the privilege of transmitting,collecting and deliver- ing telegrams in the United Kingdom. The charge for a telegram is a minimum amount of 6d. which covers 12 words, and an additional id. ior each extra word. This charge Includes delivery within the postal district if sent out for delivery from a head telegraph office, but if from a sub-office then within three miles only, beyond which a charge of 3d. per mile is made from the office door. All charges are, as far as possible, prepared by affixing stamps to telegram form. Address of person receiv- ing a telegram has to be paid for, hut sender's name if written on back of form need not be. Five figures count as one word. The cost of a reply may be prepaid. Telegrams can be repeated on payment of half the original charge, which extra payment is refunded if error is detected. Telegrams may be posted in wall or pillar boxes, or handbd to rural postmen, and are wired as soon as received at the nearest telegraph office. Late fees secure attention, in certain cases, to telegrams handed in after the usual hours. The cost of registering an abbrevi- ated address, which must consist of two words, is £1 IS. TELEGRAMS (FOREIGN AND COLONIAL) Must be written on special forms and are accepted in the language of any of the principal European countries. The rates vary, and are too numerous to detail here. POST OFFICE SAVINGS BANK.

These are established at all money order offices. In ordinary cases the annual deposit must not exceed £5O, and the total amount .C2OO. No person can have more than one account. Amounts placed in the bank on behalf of children under seven years of age cannot be withdrawn until the child reaches that age, and then only under his or her own signa- ture. The smallest amount received at a time is is., and this may take the form of 12 penny stamps affixed to a special form. Each depositor receives a bank book in which the amount of each deposit should be entered at the time of paying in. If the deposit is £1 or over, a special acknowledgment is sent from the head London office. Interest is allowed at the rate of 2i per cent. Any amount up to And inolnding £1 may be withdrawn without previous notice, on presenting a bank book. The bank book should be sent each year, postage free, on the anniversary of the date when the account was opened, to the Controller of the Savings Bank Department for purposes of checking and adding interest. The withdrawal of money can be expedited by telegraphing, the depositor paying cost of telegrams. P.O. TELEPHONE AND TRUNK LINE CHARGES.

110 TOR CAR_ AND CYCLE. REDITLATIONS.

Each one must be registered with the council of county or county borough, the fee being £1 for a motor car, and ss. for a motor cycle. A registered number is assigned to each, which it is an offence not to keep affixed when driving. Each driver must possess a license, costing 55., to be renewed every 12 months, and obtainable from the county authorities, which may be endorsed or suspended for reckless driving or certain other offences.• The highest speed at which a car may be driven on the public highways is 20 miles an hour, which is reduced to 10 miles under special local regula- tions. It is an offence under the gator Car Act (1903), to drive a car in such a way on a public highway as to be dargerous to the public, having regard to all the circumstances of the case. In the event of an accident to any person due to the presence of the motor car on the road, the driver must supply his name and address, and the owner and number of the car, under heavy • penal- ties. The driver must produce his licence at the request of a police constable.

LANCASHIRE AND CHESHIRE ASSOCIATION CLUB CHAMPIONSHIP. FINAL. WARRINGTON BULL'S HEAD v. WARRINGTON CEMETERY.—These teams met at Urmston on Saturday in the final, great interest being taken in the contest. Scores : BULL'S HEAD. CEMETERY. J Jennings Jos. Gleave 2O T Ellison E Shaw 2l John Glenve l7 J Berry 2l A Scott l9 13 Swanton ' J Bedworth 2l A J Jones 1H Fogg 16 T Bolton ' T Hackett 2l W Swanton i H Williams , W Andrews l6 G Ball, son i Albert Gleave 2l W Hill ! C Roberts 2l T Harrison

227 PRESENTING THE CUP.

The magnificent silver trophy was presented to the victors by Mr. Coombs, who had words of congratula- tion for the winning team and of sympathy for the losers. Last year, he said, be had to congratulate the Belmont Club, now he equally congratulated the Bull's Head. The victors had fully deserved the honour; they had bowled with good judgment and there was no doubt that on that day the best team had won. He only wished there had been a bigger crowd to send the winning team back. (A Voice: "There will be at Warrington," and laughter). When the presentation of the medals took place at Warrington he should be pleased to be present if he had an invitation. (Applause) He asked Mr. Albert Gleave to accept the cup as the captain of the winning team. Loud cheers were given for the victors. Mr. ALBERT GLEAVE, in answer to calls for a speech said the Bull's Head began the season with the deter- mination of winning, and now they had succeeded. They were beaten in last year's final, but they acted on Mr. Coombs' advice to "try :min." (Applause.) Alderman EVANS thanked the Cemetery team for the sportsmanlike manner in which they had met the Bull's Head side. The Cemetery Okla had laboured under great disadvantages throughout the Season in having lost players who had joined other clubs, but the club had stuck to it "in the Warrington= style, He called for three cheers for the Cemetery team, which were accorded vociferously. ODDFELLOWS' ANNUAL HANDICAP.

Great interest centred in the sixth annual handicap, in connection with the "Prince of Wales " Lodge,which took place at the Stag Inn, Walton, on Saturday, in the presence of a large crowd. The entries numbered 61. The green was in splendid condition, being a credit to Mr. J. C. Shaw, the proprietor. The games were keenly contested, and in the final, W. Roughlev defeated W. Cottaro, by 13-12. The winner thus holds the silver cup for twelve months, and also wins the beautiful gold medal, both prizes being the gift of Mr. A. H. Crosileld, M.P. Twenty other prizes are to be awarded at a smoker, which will be held at a later date. The committee who had charge of the arrangements were : —Bros. T. P. Bradshaw (chairman), J. Nlnssey, T. Wadsworth, T. Reddish, W. King, J. Willett, G. F. Freeman (deputy grand master), and E. Clayton (secretary).

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Bewsey-road Wesley Guild.

Prior to the service at Bewsey-road Church on Sunday evening, the members of the above society mot to present to their minister, the Rev. Sidney Pitt, on tho occasion of his leaving the Circuit, a group photograph of their society, taken on August Monday. Mr. Corbett presided, aud after the opening hymn and prayer, spoke in eulogistic terms of Mr. Pitt's ministry amongst them. Mr. Pitt had taken a great interest in the young people, and hadli ded them on many accasions. Mr. J. Mitchell said no minister had a deeper feeling in his heart than Mr. Pitt, and now he was leaving them he would take with him their respect and affection. Mr. Will Healey, the Guild secretary, also spoke. Mr. Pitt responded, and said the hope of the Church lay in the young people. Mrs. Pitt also spoke, and said that in saying " Good-bye " they were really saying to one another " God be by you."—On Monday evening over twenty members went for an enjoyable walk arena,' Bevrsey.

liewsey-read Wesleyan Church. Rev. Sidney Pitt's Farewell.

_

The Rev. Sidney Pitt bade farewell to the aboss Church and the Warrington Circuit, on Sunday evening last. Ile took for his text Therefore, whosoever heareth these sayings of mine, and doeth them, I will liken him into a wise MAD, which built his house upon a rock : And the rain descended, and the floods came, and the winds blow, and beat upon that house ; and it fell not ' • for it was founded upon a rock. And every- one that heareth these sayings of mine, and doeth them not, shall he likened into a foolish man, which built his house upon the sand : and the rains descended, and the winds blew, and beat upon that house ; and it fell, and great was the fall of it." (St. Matthew, vii., 21 to :27v.) He referred to the six days in which the people were exposed to the evils temptations and adverse influences of the world, and on the seventh day was commonly—only too frequently—the one poor hour in which the preacher had the opportunity to set before tee people the things of God. Take heed how ye pros ch,"—that is the voice that cs.mo to him from the far away eternity or from out of its height the voice came down. If they would listen, there was a voice, that came to them from the same infinite spaces, and from the same splendid height. " Take heed how ye hear." This responsibility of the hearer was set before them in this passage by a descrip- tion of the life history of two men. Two men there, curiously alike, and similar up to a certain point ; but the last contrasted with the other in the most tragic way. The two men, they were told, were both bearers of the word of God. He was not there to describe or theorise upon the process of how these words were preserved to them. These sayings of Jesus were assured and attested to them, not merely by documentary evidence—although there was plenty of that—but by the appeal that they make to the human heart and the human conscience, so that whenever these sayings of Jesus ,were faithfully declared the conscience of man rose up within him and said," That is true, that is the thing, and which at the bottom of ray heart I believe." It was the sayings of Jesus, that the whole thing depended upon in the last resurrection. It seemed a natural thing to spend not only six days, but to spend the Lord's Day in various kinds of pleasure, and still worse than that, in lounging and idleness instead of giving themselves the trouble to go and sit and listen to the words of blessed Christ himself. It was not for him to condemn them. It was not for parsons to say that was wrong. The very people who did it knew that it was wrong. He would not pass harsh judgement on those who were cooped up in the week, and took some opportunity on the Sunday of going forth to breath the Using air and to look upon the beautiful brilliance of nature. So far as they were personally concerned •, so far as their influence in their family was concerned, let it not be their's to drag down the habit of hearing the word of God on the Sabbath Day. Let it be their's to build up that habit and to reserve and protect it by every means in their power. "He that heareth these sayings of Mine and doeth them shall be likened unto a wise man that built his house upon a rock." The whole thing was contained in hearing the sayings of Christ and doing them. The whole of their religion was there ; salva- tion was there ; and security was th•re. Christ was the touchstone r.f humanity and lie was the majestic person by whose relationship their ultimate destiny was to be decided.

In conclusion the rev, gentleman said that they had now come to a finish. What a dream it seemed since three years ago he first stood there and looked upon their faces. What changes for them and for him in that little space of time. He-Looked upon that congre- gation with how different a view. Then it was as if the place were a blank ; it meant nothing as he looked upon it. But now there wcre scores and hundreds of faces that he knew. Ile had married many, by which their lives were inextricably bound together, never to be separated until death parted them. lie hind one to another, husbands to wives ; make each other happy and help each other. There and elsewhere he had taken ' little children into his arms and administered to them that water whit]] indicated the rromise given to the Holy Spirit. Fathers and mothers, watch over your children, and bring them up in the nurture of the Lord. He had sat with many at the bed-side of the dying and he hoped, that he and they would meet them again. Ile did not believe that this world was all. lie could not live if he thought this world were all. The last words he had to say to them were, " Hear the words of Christ and obey them ; for 'whosoever heareth these sayings of mine and doeth them I will liken unto a wise man who built his house upon a rock." The Hey. Sidney Pitt's departure for Leeds is greatly regretted by all those in the Warrington circuit with , whom he came in contact.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Authors of letters intended for publication ML.tht in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith.

Warrington Chamber of Commerce and the Daylight Saving Bill. Sir,—The Warrington Chamber of Commerce at their last meeting are reported to have resolved unanimously to support and petition in favour of the so-called Daylight Saving Bill. This Bill which is promoted by a private Member of Parliament purports to he a measure introduced for the purpose of economising artificial ligat by diraini;hing the necessity for its use daring the summer months of I the year, but the means by which it proposes to secure' this object, namely, putting the clocks throughout the I country out of synchronism with Greenwich Mean Solar I time is objectionable in the extreme, and should the , Bill ever have the misfortune to become law, it will, as ! the editor of one of our technicel journals has tersely I expressed it," breed more passive resisters than all thei Education Acts have ever done." Even if it could be admitted that the method proposed by the Bill was a legitimate one, I the proposal could not have been put forward at is more inopportune time, just when all the , leading civilized nations of the earth have adopted Greenwich mean time as the basis from which I time all over the world is reckoned. This consnmma- I tion has been brought about by the superior excellence of the scientific work that has been carried on chiefly by Great Britain during the last two centuries, and to the extreme accuracy with which the position of the , Greenwich Meridian has been determined. Patriotic 1 British subjects, therefore, may be excused if they feel, a sense of shame or evert disgust, that Great Britain itself should now propose to throw Greenwich mean ' time over, as soon as this desirable object has been secured. Let it be granted that certain industries might benefit by work being commenced earlier in the morning and finished earlier in the evening during the summer months, that end could surely be attained without resort- ing to such a barbarous expedient as putting the clocks of the country out of harmony with nature. When the project was first announced by its author it appeared so monst; ously absurd and impracticable to many of us that little notice was taken of it, but very much to their surprise the proposal has received such a large measure of support, that it must now be taken more seriously, and it looks as if we may have to prepare to face the confusion into which we shall be plunged should the bill become law. It should never be lost sight of, in handling a question like this, that there are certain spheres in which rigorous adherence to Mean Solar Time is of paramount importance. For example ; the safe navigation of our mercantile marine and naval fleets is dependant on this being recorded and maintained with the greatest accuracy, and the observatories that have been erected throughout the length and breadth of our land cannot have their time interfered with, without frustrating one of the primary purposes for which they have been called into existence, and the inconvenience and con- fusion that will result from having two different methods of reckoning running side by side far more than outweigh any supposed advantage to be derived from the change. In addition to this our prestige among the nations will undoubtedly suffer, for it must not be assumed that other countries will continue to base their reckon- ing on Greenwich Mean Time when the coantry of its origin has discarded it. But the mischief is not likely to end there. Few people seem to he aware that another project of a still more drastic character is on foot, namely, a proposal to alter the calendar itself. It is proposed that on aid after the year 1916, or such other date as Parliameat may ordain, the year shall always begin co the first day of the week ; in other words, that the first day of January shall always be Sunday, and this is to be accomplished by having one week of eight days in each ordinary year, and two such weeks in each leap year. There seems little difficulty in forecasting ,abat the effect of such an enactment would be were it to become law. To nations who preserved the orthodox reckoning this conntry would present the extraordinary spectacle of its day of rest occurring successively on all the days of the week, as the years passed by, and one can scarcely conceive of a professedly Christian community accept- ing such an alternative, for it would most certainly put an end to the public observance of the Lord's Gay as a Divine institution, for it would no longer be the Lord's Day, but simply the day appointed by Government. Yet this scheme has now been before the public for some time, and is being energetically canvassed by its advocates who appear to be hopeful that it will one day be the law of the land. To tamper with the measurement of time or its nomenclature is a much more serious matter than many people suppose. More particularly is this the case now that scientific accuracy has been brought to such perfection and one wonders whether those bodies, who have petitioned in favour of such a bill as that in question have given any really serious and intelligent consideration to the subject, and I have grave doubts whether, in framing the resolution to petition in favour of the bill and passing it nem. con.,the members of the Warrington Chamber of Commerce realized the gravity of the step they were taking, or were alive to the farreaching consequences involved in their action. I enclose my card and subscribe myself.—Yours

A FELLOW OF TFIE ROYAL ASTRONOMICAL SOCIETY. August ISth, 19°S. .

ROYAL COURT THEATRE. "The Mysteries of the Thames."

Excitement runs high at the Royal Curt Theatre this \reek, "The Mysteries of the Thames" presenting a succession of powerful sensations. The audiences are deeply moved by the chequered fortunes of Richard Brandon and his wife, and interest is act allowed to Rag for a moment. One cf the principal scenes graphically depicts life on board a convict ship, with the torture of ,prisoners which was common in "the good old days.' It is clue to Mr. Augustine Knight and his company to say that they give a painstaking ard entirely successful representa- tion of the play. Mr. Knight himself appeals to all by his fine portrayal of Richard Brandon, his telling elocution and manly bearing eminently fitting him for the part. Mr. H. Bonhote Wilson earns the execration of the " gods " for his realistic acting as the remorseless Captain James Denbigh, alias " The Hawk," whose villainy causes all the trouble. Miss Eva Trevenna wins all hearts asl -Kate Brandon and Miss Ella Vane submits a clever study of the dual role of Grace Rochdale and Helen Price. Mr. Edward E. Swinton as Bill Bosco, a burglar with a " winning " way, and Tom J. Taylor as Peter Waggles are responsible for some capital humorous interludes. Miss Elsie Jones, a promising juvenile, does well as the child Doris, and Miss Marie E. Robertson is an admirable Emma Seaton. NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME.

_ Next week Mr. Percy Norman's Company will appear in the great domestic and military play " A Woman worth winning," wbich is daringly conceived /and contains many stirring and pathetic incidents as well as most welcome humour. Mr. A. B. Mackay, the author,w ill appear as General Borinski, Misss Ida Mackay as Katerina Kloss, Miss Chrissie Dunbar as Roma Pauleff and Mr. Carthage Caldcleugh as Ivan Bardoff. There will be gorgeous scenery and uniforms and an explosion scene will present something novel and exciting in the way of stage effects,

A TEMINT SUED.

Damage Done By Dogs.

—At the Warrington County Court on Thursday before is Honour Judge Shiress Will, K.C., Mrs. Helena Jenkins, Burbanks, Fairfield-road, Latchford, sued Ainslie George Bond, Victoria-avenue, Grappenhall, for 7 les. for damage done to the semi-detached villa, Brightholme, Fairfield-road, Latchford, during his tenancy.—Mr. T. S. Steel appPared for the plaintiff anc. Mr. C H Unsworth for the defendant. Mr. Steel said the house was let to the defendant at £25 per annum and rates and taxes, and before he took possession on June 17th, 1937. some .£5 was spent in alterations. He left on April 10th of the following year and the house was then found to be in a shocking condition, tiles being broken, holes being knocked in the walls to insert large wooden plugs, probably for picture hanging, and other damage being done: Defendant's wife seemed to be a lover of clogs, and during their tenancy they :kept some half-dozen dogs. It was alleged that the animals had gnawed and clawed the French casements in the drawing.room as well as some of the doors. Evidence in support of plaintiff's case was given by plaintiff, Francis Arthur Jenkins, her husband; Albert Worrall, builder and contractor, Fairfield-road, Stock- ton Heath ; James Adams, master painter and decorator, 722, Knutsford-road ; and Mrs. M. Davies, 5, Fre&rick-street, Latchford. Defendant admitted that damage had been done to the casements by a dog but said 10s. would cover it and be bad paid that amount into Court, as well as ss. for other odds and ends. Mr. Bond contended that some of the tiles were damaged when he went into the house and that the statements as to the other damage were exaggerated. Defendant's case was supported by Mrs, Mary G. Bond, his wife, Mrs. Mary hewitt, 13, Sandhill-terrace, Wash-lane, Edith G ri friths, Birkenhead, Mabel Boardman, Timperley. and Mrs. Haddock.—His Honour gave judgment for the plaintiff for £0 and costs.

COUNCILLOR sToTrs DEPARTURE.

FAREWELL ADDRESS AND PRESENTATION.

Meeting in Orford Ward.

Councillor Frei Stott will'set sail for New Zealand today (Saturday). He delivered his final address to his constituents in Orford Ward at a meeting held at the top of Longford-street on Tuesday evening. There was a large attendance, Councillor Ball presiding. Councillor STOTT said he was even more firmly con- vinced than he was when he entered the Council that I Socialism was necessary before the bulk of I the workers would be able to live in decency and i comfort. His ideal as a Councillor had been that what I the people socially needed they should socially own I and although he had not been able to achieve much in Ithe direction of Socialism he thought he might claim to bare done some pioneer work. When seeking the support of the electors he said that although he did not j promise any reduction in the rates he would try to j safeguard public expenditure as far as possible. The j standard of municipal government was increasing and Corporations would have to spend more money as years went by. If the rates were to be reduced they would ; have to find new sources of revenue. (Ilear, j hear). Councillor Stott referred to his efforts Ito obtain an expression of opinion from the Council in favour of a municipal coal supply and said he contended that by such a system the inhabitants would be saved from £15,000 to £16,000 per annum. He Imaintained that in conducting Corporation undertak- j ins, the public convenience and not profit should be j the first consideration. Councillor Stott pointed out

===== 0002601_19080822_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNCILLOR F. STOTT, J.P.

that ho had always done his best to obtain improved lighting and paving of streets and he claimed that the paving in that ward had been pushed on much more rapiday of late years. So strong had been his agitation that owners of property had come to him to try and persuade him to desist, so that the streets could remain unpaved and their money would be saved. He was one of those who believed that the streets and passages ought to b 3 paved and sewered before the houses were let but when four years ago he forward a motion to impose that condition he did not obtain a single sup- porter. With regard to open spaces and recreation grounds, he was not well satisfied with the conditions in ()fiord Ward and he had for some time been advocating the provision of a bath and a howling green on the Orford-lane recreation ground. Such an innovation would prove a great boon not only to Orford Ward, but to St. John's and Bewsey Wards. At present the dis- tricts in which the people who did the dirtiest and hardest kinds cf work had to live, afforded the least facilities for washing. He hoped some day they would be able to persuade the Council to enforce bye-laws which would compel builders of property to place a bath in every house, but pending that the Corporation should provide reasonable facilities for every district at a cheap rate—in fact he believed baths ought to be provided free of charge. When he came before the electors he promised to advocate the abolition of school fees. They had not attained that yet, but a start had been made on the right lines, as they had two Council schools in which no fees were charged. He believed the time was not far distant when all the schools would be freed.l Socialists, however, believed in a much larger scheme than that. All education, from the elemen- tary school to the university should be free to children who would benefit by it. Councillor Stott alluded to his advocaey of a trade union clause in all Corporation contracts, contending that it would have put all firms on the same footing and have avoided unfair competition. At present they had a clause which was not sstistactory to socialists and trades unionists, and moreover it was necessary that they should send men to the Council who would insist on its being enforced. He had on many occasions found that work was being given to contractors who were not adhering with the conditions and in almost every case he had either been able to compel the employer to pay the proper rate or be was instrumental in having the contract given to someone else. Ue had used his influence in support of the direct employment of labour by the Council as he believed they could do a great deal better without the contractor. The successful painting of the market by men directly employed by the Corporation proved the force of his contention. He had consistently advocated a redaction in the price cf gas, for he held that the consumer should have the benefit of the large profits instead of always devoting them to the relief of rates. With regard to the question of the minimum wage, although he was able to prove that it was impossible for a man and his family to live in decency and comfort on less than 245. a week, the Council refused to adopt a minimum of that amount. Subsequently, on the pro- position of another member, they established a mini- mum rate of 215., and he supported that with the assurance that he would not rest content until it had been raised to 245. In conclusion Councillor Stott claimed that he had done his utmost to fulfil his promises to the electors and it would be impossible for the Council to go back upon what had been done in the direction of social reform. Ha believed the ratepayers would, as years went by, ask for a higher standard of local government and if they continued to return members of the Independent Labour Party to represent them, reforms would be much more speedily brought about than if they sent Tories snd Liberals to the Council. He asked the electors of Orford Ward to rally to the support of Mr. James Hampson who had consented to become the candidate in his (Councillor Stott's) place. (Applause.) Mr. H. WeaDeres moved: "That this meeting of residents of Orford Ward tenders its best thanks to Councillor Stott for his five years valuable services as a public representative and wishes him and Mrs. Stott every success in their new sphere of life. ' He said all would agree that Councillor Stott had not been in the Council for any self-seeking purposes but had always been animated by concern for the public good, his sympathies being with the poor and oppressed. (Applause.) Mr. E. SHEPHERD seconded and they would be extremely sorry to lose Councillor Stott's servicts. Mr. HAWTHORNE, as one who had frequently opposed the principles advocated by Councillor Stott, heartily associated himself with the resolution. Their disagree- ments had been in a friendly spirit and he firmly believed that Councillor Stott had done everything he could, to the best of his knowledge, for the good of the community. (Applause). The resolution was carried unanimously. Councillor STOTT in reply said he had put in 491 attendances at Council and committee meetings during the four years and eight months that he had been a member. Before the meeting closed the audience joined in singing " Auld Lang Syne "and "For he's a jolly good fellow "and gave hearty cheers for Councillor and Mrs. S to tt.

PRESENTATION AT THE TAPESTRY WORKS.

and

On Thursday morning the employees of the above works assembled to present Councillor Stott with a handbag, which had unanimously been subscribed for, as a token of their esteem for him on his departure to Nev.v Zealand.—Miss E. JEFFERSON, who made the presentation, spoke of Mr. Stott's association with them for so many years, and how all had worked so well together. On behalf of all she wished him every success and that he would meet friends in the new country, as he had in the old.

_ Mr. STOTT replied, thanking them for their kindly expression in the gift just presented, siying be would treasure it very much, as it showed all differences were sunk at a time like the present. He would always remember his associations with them, and he could only say that he hoped they would have as happy a future as they had wished him.—Mr. HAROLD E. C. LONDON, on behalf of the men, said that they all greatly regretted the departure from them of such a personality as Mr. Stott. He would be greatly missed, especially as he worked so well with them, and was always ready to give a helping hand. He had worked hard both at work and outside in his leisure time, or he would not have attained to the high and honourable position he held in the town. It was far from pleasant to lose such a friend, but their loss was someone else's gain.—After singing " Auld Lang Syne," all dispersed to their different departments.

Anything that might be received after Councillor Stott's departure would he paid over to Mrs. Stott who was remaining in England for some time. Many letters of kindly 1 appreciation had come to hand, the writers includ- ing many who were very much opposed to Councillor Stott's principles but who had learnt to respect the manner in which he had put forth his views. The picture of the Town Council ,which had been obtained at the express desire of Councillor Stott as a continual memento of his association with that body, was inscribed : " Presented to Councillor Stott, J.P., as a token of respect and esteem on his departure to New Zealand, August 22nd, 1003." The books also bore an inscription stating that they were presented by Socialist comrades;trado union brethren, and other friends and well wishers. (Applause.) Mr. Joseph Podmore, jun., said that as the oldest member of the Warrington Independent Labour Party be had been asked to make the presentation. He was pleased to know that Councillor Stott in the course of his public career bad won such a good Demean over the town. It had been almost a surprise to him to receive letters of appreciation from men diametrically opposed to their friend in his principles, and he believed one of the causes of *at appreciation was that Councillor Stott bad acted as a straightforward, honest man. (Applause.) He went into the Council under no false colours and ho had steadfastly pursued his path. There were some whom the blandishments of social position might turn aside, but Fred Stott had kept Fred Stott right through the chapter. ( Applause). Ile bad sot a very high example of what a workingman could do in public work. There had been no harder worker for the trade unionist case in Warrington than Fred Stott—eipplause)—and he stood to-day pre- eminent as a worker and as a man. It was exactly fourteen years that night since he (the speaker) and Councillor Stott first became acquainted and it was also the fourteenth birthday of the II arrirgton branch of the Independent Labour Party. Councillor Stott's Socialism had been to him, as it .was to all true Socialists, an inspiration. Ho had seized hold of the true spirit of Socialism and it had been his guide and help right through his career. They were very sorry to part with him but they knew he would be received with the right hand of fellowship by the Socialists in New Zealand. He (Mr. Podmore) firmly believed that they would eventually hear of Councillor Stott occupy- ing some eminent position in Nevy Zealand. In making the presentation Mr. Podmore wished Councillor Stott long life, good health, prosperity and happiness. (Applause). e Mr. J. A. SEDDON', M.P., said he had travelled some- thing like 400 miles in order to join in sending their comrade away with a message of hope and cheer. No one would appreciate the loss to the district more than he (Mr. Seddon). When he was the victim of out- rageous fortune, when minds that were polluted by political prejudice were trying to besmirch his personal character,Fred Stott was there to defend it. (Applause.) During the memorable struggle in the Newton division, in season and out of season he found in Councillor Stott a valuable ally and a trusted friend. There was no friend whose loss he would feel more keenly. The sacrifices which Councillor Stott and his wife had made for the work in Warrington could not be estimated, but their friend had the satisfaction of seeing some of the fruits of his labour. Fourteen years ago he joined in the formation of a small branch—a spurned section of the community—and to-day he steed, apart from his political views, probably the most respected man in Warrington. (Applause.) It had been his (Mr. Seddon's) good fortune to hear Councillor Stott's praises sung by those who were uncompromisingly opposed to him politically. The Parliamentary repre- sentative of Warrington had seen fit to send his contri- bution as an appreciation of the strenuous work of , Councillor Stott. (Applause.) He went to New Zealand with recommendations of which any man might be proud. lie had a recommendation from the revered leader of the Socialist and labour movement. Mr. Kier Hardie —(applause)—and also from Mr. Ramsey Macdonald. Only that afternoon he (Mr. Seddon) exchanged a few words at a railway station with the Chairman of the Labour party, Mr. Arthur Henderson, who asked him to convey his appreciation of Councillor Stott's , efforts on behalf of the Labour movement and to wish him " God-speed " in his new sphere of life. (Applause.) It was something to be able to leave one's friends and one's foes with such a testimonial. It ought to be an inspiration not only to Councillor Stott himself but to every young man in the movement. When Councillor Stott entered the Town Council every- one looked at him askance and treated him with ridicule. lie had, however, consistently upheld the standard he took up from the start and had defended his position, which was solitary for a long time, and the very men who opposed him most tenaciously now joined in appreciating his sincerity, honesty and steadfastness. Councillor Stott was going to another country and he (Mr. Seddon) would that he were fortunate enough to go with him. He believed the possibilities were infinitely greater, so far as Now Zealand was concerned. New Zealand had for many years been in the forefront of social reform. One thing Councillor Stott would certainly not see in the same aggravated form that we saw it in this country—the appalling poverty that surrounded us everywhere. He would not see the unfed, unshod, strayed school child. He would see the industrial veterans of toil at the age of sixty-five in receipt not of charity but of a pension at the hands of the Government, sufficient to keep them in decency and comfort. He would find in that Colony, notwithstand- ing all that had been done, an inspiration to help them to further deeds and to realize, as probably we soon would in this country, that the only hope of the worker was to possess all the means of production, distribution and exchange for the use of the community and not for the use of the exploiter alone. (Applause.) Councillor Stott had stood out as one of the pioneers of thought. He might not have been one of the most brilliant, but he was one of the most consistent. He had not been hair-brained. Every attitude he had taken up had been the outcome of reflection. It might be, with the quick transit and the growing forces of Socialism and Labour throughout the world, that after a few years of honourable toil and distinction in the Colonies,they might have the opportunity of welcoming Councillor Stott back as a visitor, relating the experi- ences he had gained in his new home. (Applause.) It was their ardent prayer and wish that he would have a pleasant voyage, that he would on his arrival in the Colony be welcomed by friends, and that when he was surrounded by his family he would be able to take up the work he had left here, and go forward with the determination to conquer because his principles were eternal, just and right. The man who did that would have the highest reward that could come to any human being—the reward of his own conscience, that in his day and generation be tried to make the world better and to serve his fellow men. (Applause.) Messrs. C. Lee, E. Horrocks, W. Pook, T. Plumb, E. Randles, W. Lowe, and W. Richardson (president of the Trades and Labour Council) also added a few words. Councillor LORD said it gave him great pleasure to be present, as a co-worker of Councillor Stott's on the Town Council. It had always been a pleasure to sit with him in committee. lie had found Councillor Stott to be one of the true representatives of the people and whenever he (Councillor Lord) had asked him to go in a certain direction which he thought was right and Councillor Stott had seen his way, he had willingly done it. (Applause). He hoped Councillor Stott would have a pleasant journey and health and success in his new home. (Applause). The CHAIRMAN said Councillor Stott had brought a soundness of judgment, a considered opinion, and a sanity of mind to hear in the consideration of public questions, quite equal if not superior to many of those who were non-Socialists. (Applause). Councillor STOTT, on rising to reply, was greeted with loud cheers. He said he had always prided himself upon being a fighter, but he had also tried to fight fairly and to be courteous. He could assure them that he felt very keenly the fact that he was about to leave War- rington. Seven or eight months ago he had no such thought, but was looking forward to the time when Councillor Ball and be would have more Socialist colleagues to help them in their work on the Town Council. He had, however, to make the choice between Birkenhead and New Zealand. He had not seen New Zealand but he had been to Birkenhead, and he thought those who knew Birkenherd and had learnt anything about New Zealand would agree that he had made a wise selection. (Laughter.) Councillor Stott caused amusement by narrating air incident which happened when he came to Warrington as a stranger sixteen years ago. DEPARTURE TO-DAY.

Councillor Stott will leave Central Station for Liverpool at 9.30 this (Saturday) morning and is sure to receive a hearty" send-oa." He will set sail from Liverpool in the "Surrey."

The wedding took place on Thursday at Christ I Church, Latchford, of Miss Elizabeth Maltman Ewart, I eldest daughter of Mrs. J. A. Ewart, RO3O Lea, Latch- ford, to Mr. George McLean Milne, son of the late Mr. and Mrs. J. Milne, of St. Vincent, West Indies. The Rev. G. C. Briggs, M.A., officiated in the presence of a large company of relatives and friends. The bride was given away by Alderman W. J. Forshaw, and looked very charming in a handsome dress of cream satin, trimmed with chiffon and embroidered lace. She wore a pearl and emerald pendant,and carried a bouquet of white roses and lilies, the gifts of the bridegroom. Her attendant bridesmaids were the Misses Annie and Beatrice Ewart (sisters of the bride), who wore saxe silk eolienne, trimmed with lace and net. Their blquets were of yellow marguerites and they wore gold and pearl brooches, presents from the bridegroom. The best man was Mr. J. S. Cairns, of Cairo. Miss I Reynolds officiated at the organ, and played music I appropriate to the service. After the ceremony a reception was held at Rose Lea, the newly-married couple departing later for Scotland, where the honey- moon is being spent. Mrs. Milne travelled in a blue cloth costume, trimmed with silk braid, and wore a heliotrope bat.

Langton—Barton.

The marriage was solemnized at Friars' Green Chapel, in the presence of a large congregation, on Thursday, of Mr. George Henry Langton, son of Mr. William Langton, of Woodville, and Miss Mary Bradford Barton, daughter of Councillor and Mrs. Thomas Barton, of " Sunnymede," Lovely-lane, Warrington. The-service was conducted by Councillor James Evans. The hymn 'The Voice that breathed o'er Eden" was sung, Mr. Tanner presiding at the organ, and the bride and bridegroom left the chapel to the strains of Mendelssohn's " Wedding March." The bride, who was given away 'by her father, was attired in a beautiful Empire dress of ninon-de-soie over silk glace trimmed with Brussels lace, with veil surmounted with spray of heather. She carried a shower bouquet of lilies, orchids, and chrysanthemums, the gift of the bridegroom. The bridesmaids were the Misses Frances and Lily Barton (sisters of the bride), who wore pretty Wedgewood blue Empire dresses of crêpe-de-chine trimmed with silk lace, silk sashes, and embroidered with narrow braid, and hats of silk lace and real panama. Their bouquets were composed of rich pink carnations and were the gifts of the bridegroom. The bride's mother wore a rich black silk dress relieved with a pretty vest of lace, and bonnet trimmed • with tiny roses and white osprey. She carried a shower bouquet of roses. Mr. J. T. Barton (brother of the bride) discharged the dnties of best man. The reception was held at the Winmarleigh Cafe, and was largely attended. Later Mr. and Mrs. G. IT. Langton left for North Wales where the honeymoon is being spent. The bride's travelling dress was a brown tweed costume with hat to match. Many handsome presents were received.
