PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

Harry Weldon, famous as "Stuffy, the goal-keeper" in Karno's football match, heads the bill at the Palace this week and provides some excellent fun. He narrates the story of a cheese of tremendous longevity and a chicken of doubtful quality and also impersonates that desperate highwayman, Dick Turpin. He talks of valiant deeds but cuts a sorry figure when he has an opportunity of putting his bravery to the test and the audience is kept in a state of hilarity by his doings. Another laughable feature is the absurdity "The silly burglar" by the Waldrons. The cracksman is exactly as described, for he sets to work on a tempting safe, with a commotion sufficient to ' rouse the neighbourhood. The gallant men in blue are soon on the scene but make a poor show, and the silly one has quite the best of matters. Helen Martin delights everyone with her refined singing, a number of familiar airs being interpreted with taste and feeling. Baker and Balfe are extremely skilful performers on the xylophone and play the overture to" Maritana " with fine effect. Mr. George W. Baker is a capital dancer and trips about gaily while the lady plays " The harp that once " oa the xylophone. Carr and Rose present a capital operetta which tells of a little domestic misunderstanding. R,osie Archer's mimicry is a popular feature, her impersonations of Vesta Tilley,Harry Lander,Vesta Victoria, and Victoria *Maks being very clover. Renee Grahame, an accomplished character vocalist, gives a dramatic rendering of " Playmates " and Hatry Sims scores a success as a light comedian, Mr. Widop's orchestra plays some excellent selections, and Raymond's 13io-Tableaux bring the programme to a close with a humorous set entitled " Catch me if you can."

MAGINN, J.P.

John Maginn—to give him the title by which he loved to be called, and by which, in fact, every friend, if not indeed every person in Warrington, called him— was the son of William Maginn, an Ulster tenant- farmer and cattle dealer, and was born at Dromore,,Co. Tyrone,on January 27th,I859,being the eldest of a family of nine. His opportunities of education were scanty, occasional attendance at a rural National School until about nine years of age being all that was available to him. His uncle, however, was the local schoolmaster, and a genuine love of knowledge was implanted in him early by both his parents. Edacatim was not then compulsory, and ho left school entirely at that early age to help in the work of his father's farm. He remained at home till he was 18 years of age, but poverty, never vary far from the Irish tenant-farmer, rested with cruel force on his class in the late seventies. The commencement of the importation of American frozen meat and live stock into Great Britain utterly ruined the Ulster farmers and stock-breeders for the time.

Young John Maginn's sense of filial duty, which remained fresh and sincere down to the day of his death, and the feeling of responsibility as the eldest of a large impelled him to make special efforts to assist his parents, and with this object in view he endeavoured to get to Australasia on the Colonial Assisted Imigra- tion Scheme. Just as he thought he had succeeded in this, the Colonial Agents General in London announced that their Governrsents had withdrawn the scheme. The determined youth, seeing his hopes in that direction failed, came to England to look for employment. On the first day of his arrival a chance meeting in the train with Mr. R. T. Fairclough, of Warrington, resulted in that gentleman taking a kindly interest in the smart youth and offering him work at the Mersey Mills. Mr. Maginn often referred to this incident as provi- dential, for he had no intention of staying in Warrington when he set out from Liverpool. Since he came into prominence he often referred to Warrington as the town of his adoption, and spoke with his friends of the large consequences which flow from small causes. When, 30 years ago, Mr. Fairclough, entering the train at Widnes, kindly spoke to the young raw Irish lad and engaged him to do humble work for him in Warrington, he was really playing the part of initiator in a remote sense of several successful businesses of the present day, and determined for John Maginn definitely that Warrington was to be the scone of his life's work. But he only remained in Messrs. Pairciough's employ for a few months, as his restless, ambitions spirit urged him ;to advance. After returning to Ireland for a short I time, ho came again to Warrington and got an appoint- ment as a canvasser or travelling salesman. It was while serving in this capacity he came so much into contact with the working classes, and formed such a strong attachment for them and deep and sincere interest in their welfare. He had always looked upon them as his earliest and kindest friends.

About this time, 1882: h-e—sen-t-To-; several of his brothers to join him in Warrington, and a short time later he accepted an appointment under a sewing machine company as manager of their depot at Bury. His love for Warrington, and his belief in it as a busi- ness centre; led him to close the Bury depot and t o open the present branch of that firm in Warrington. The control of this business was not enough, how- ever, to absorb the whole of his energies, and, with the consent of his employers, he began the livery stable business in Arpley-street, and later in Sankey-street. Arpley-street at that time was only just emerging from the country or waste land stage, and it is now remem- bered that when his friends joked him about beginning I business outside the town, as they called it, Mr. Maginn strongly asserted the town would come his way some day. Events have proved his assertion true, for now almost every public institution in the borough is within a stone's throw of the location of his first adventure in trade. _ _

His snccess was rapid and continuous from this point. His early experience of live stock suggested to him to import superior carriage horses from Ireland, and while he fiercely repudiated the appellation of " horse dealer," he met with substantial encourage- ment, being patronized by many of the nobility and gentry of the district. The late Colonel Twentyman was a special patron of his and was instrumental in introducing him to many of his important customers. He was honoured with commissions from Mr. G. L. Dewhurst, the Hon. John Cross, lairs. Usher (of Arley Hall), the Countess of Meath, and numerous others. It might be mentioned that Mr. Maginn had a letter from the late Mr. Thomas Fletcher, in which that gentleman complimented him on some business he had done for him, and added the following characteristic sentence : —" I used to say, Maginn, I never know an honest horse dealer ;.I cannot say it now."

But John Maginn was never a "horsey man," he probably never bought a copy of a sporting paper nor knew the name of a Darby winner. He early saw the significance of motor cars and the advers3 influence they would have on the price and use of carriage horses and he decided when they came into being first, to withdraw to a large extent from the livery business and embark in some other less liable) to fluctuations. The result is seen in his large furniture establishments in Warrington, Bolton and elsewhere, and also in the Alliance Box Works, which was established by him and his brothers co-operatively. Had he lived, Warrington would have gained another trade eventually, for he contemplated the addition of the manufacture of bent- wood chairs to his other undertakings as soon as he had created a connection large enough to consume the product of such a factory. Mr. Maginn took little part in public affairs (and he was never a political partizan in the usual acceptance of the term) until a few years ago. He believed in a man demonstrating his capacity to conduct his own business successfully before he sought to direct the affairs of the community. It was in Julv; 1303, that he first attempted to enter the Town Council, trying con- clusions in St. John's Ward with Mr. Henry Thornton, and, like most aspirants in their first attempt, he suffered a heavy defeat. The following November he again contested the same ward, and secured election by a record majority. That majority was no less than Gil and was recorded against Mr. Ashley, run by the Non- trading party. Three years later, Mr. Maginn was ,himself defeated by Mr. T. S. Steel, Conservative, by

no less than 522. He tried to re-enter the Council, via St. Austin's Ward, in January of this year, a vacancy being caused by the elevation of Mr. Thomas Burton to the aldermanic bench, but he was defeated by Mr. Broadhurst, Conservative by 200 votes. There can be no two opinions as to the usefulness of Mr. Magian while he was in the Council Chamber, for though he appeared to some ta be a little too abrupt in his methods he was thoroughly fearless and independent, and his speeches were often illumined with touches of genuine Irish wit. After the last General Election he was made a borough magistrate, and, as the Mayor so well expressed it on Monday, ha ever showed a desire to temper [justice with mercy. _

. John Maginn always stood as an Independent, and never allowed himself to be described as " belonging " to any political party, though his convictions were mostly Liberal. At the last General Election he was among the foremost supporters of the present Member for the Borough, Mr. AR. Crosfleld, and particularly of his Home Rule and Free Trade view. But he was somewhat of a free lance on other questions. He did not agree with any of the recent education proposals, contending that education should be a national under- taking, not a local or municipal one. He often argued , that the closely populated districts' where rates were high and children numerous could not afford to educate the young generation eticiently and that the cost of sustaining a generous system of national education should be distributed pro rata on property over the country as a whole. While he lacked in support of the resent Government's declared education schemes, he supported their Licensing Bill. Only quite recently he addressed several meetings ; in fact, he addressed several meetings during his illness when he was not, at all fit for such a task. Mr. Maginn was always an enthusiastic supporter of every proposal designed to advance the well-being of the working-class—" my class " as he called them—but ho was no believer in coddling, or pauperizing treatment for theta. He wanted to see workingmen freed from evil influences and environment, and he had confidence that, given proper opportunities, they would of their own capacity and native aspirations work out their own salvation.

He was a voracious reader, diffuse and unmethodical rather than studious. It was curious to hear him expound theories and convey information of the most unexpected kind for a man of his upbringing. He was full of facts relating to all manner of subjects, and it was by his peculiar and extensive reading, and by his actual contact with life here and in continental countries' that he became the self-reliant man he was. He had aforemost admiration for Thomas Hardy, among novelists, and it might be said that that author's book, "The Mayor of Casterbridge," had an influence in shaping Mr. Maginn's own career, had it only been written a decade earlier than it was.

Mr. Maginn married in 1890 Mahalah, the second 'daughter of George and Thyrza Voase, of Martin-in- Holderness, Yorkshire. This lady proved a true help- meet to him in all his business undertakings, and with- out doubt contributed by her energy and ability to his success as a trader. He leaves her to survive him, to bring up their family of five—two sons and three daughters—all of whom are still of tender ape. Mr. Maginn's love of children was unique. Who an War- rington does not remember the colossal " Kidies' Picnics "to Lymna during the years he was the St. John's representative in the Council. . .

_ Altogether he was the one of most remarkable Irish- men who ever came into Warrington and took a part in its corporate life, and proudly claimed it as the city of his adoption.

As a in-ark of respect to the deceased gentlemen the flags at the Town Hall and the Liberal Club were dis- played at half mast.

THE FUNERAL. Striking evidence of the respect in which the late Mr. Maginn was held by all classes of the community was afforded on Wednesday when the funeral took place at the Warrington Cemetery. The streets which the procession traversed were lined with people and at the Cemetery a large concourse had gathered to pay a last tribute to one who had played such a worthy part in the public and commercial life of the town. The mem- bers of the Corporation and magistrates met at the Town Hall and joined the corthge at Raddon Court,the deceased's late residence. For some time before the funeral, the bell of St. Mary's Church, Buttermarket- street, was tolled. The procession was headed by a detachment of police in charge of Inspector Smith and the representatives of public bodies followed in carriages. The coffin was covered with beautiful wreaths and a large number of additional floral tributes were conveyed in a brougham. The last rites were performed by the Rev. J. H. Roche, of Our Lady's Church, Latchford.

The funeral arrangements were carried out for Messrs Magian Bros., by Mr. G. H. Whitney. COUNCILLOR STOTT'S TRIBUTE. In the course of his foal address to the electors of Orford Ward on Tuesday evening, Councillor Stott said that although the late Mr. Maginn was such a strong opponent of his on the question of trade unionism, they were very good friends and only three weeks ago had a long and interesting conversation on municipal Govern- ment. He (Councillor Stott) had had occasion to differ very strongly with Mr. Maginn, but he could assure that meeting that their departed friend hai many good qualities, and he believed that had Mr. Maginn again been returned to the Council he would have adopted a different attitude with regard to trade union conditions. They all regretted that a man in the prime of life who appeared to be so strong some months ago, should pass away so suddenly, and their deepest sympathy was with the widow and family.

MAGISTERIAL REFERENCES. Before commencing the business at the Borough Police Court on Monday, The Mayon (Alderman Smethurst) said it was his unfortunate duty to have to announce from that Bench the death of one of their brother magistrates, Mr. John Maginn. Mr. Maginn had only been a short time in the office of magistrate of the borough, but they all believed sincerely that during the time he had fulfilled that office he had endeavoured, according to his own conscience, to adminster justice with mercy. He (the Mayor) was sure that the feelings of the Bench and the eel ings of the Court were that the greatest sympathy f hould he expressed to his dear widow and family, and sll connected with him. a Mr. W. ROBERTS said he would like to say, as one of the juniors upon that bench, how deeply he mourned the loss they had sustained. On Monday of list week Mr. Maginn sat by his side, and it was patent to all that he was in great bodily suffering, and all felt that his time in this world was exceedingly short. fie looked a pathetic figure, and he (Mr. Roberts) thought that Mr. Maginn profoundly believed that his attendance there last Monday would probably be his last. He told Mr. Wright and himself how greatly he feared that his time was very short. He was profoundly attached to the dear old town of Warrington. He (the speaker.) well remem- bered that In one of his addresses, Mr. Magma stated how many years ago, he tramped into the town a poor, labour- ing lad, looking out for employment. That employment was eventually found, and from that day he had never turned back, but by persistent hard work, indomitable courage and perseverance, prosperity seemed to have dawned upon him, and just at the time when success seemed to be within his grasp an insidious disease had taken hold of him and carried him away. Their tenderest sympathies went out that morning to his bereaved widow and children. He commended them to Him who had promised to be a husband to the widow and a father to the fatherless children. Mr. S. Wright said he had been closely associated with Mr. Magian, and he felt his loss very much. He did not think there was any man jg the town who was more greatly attached to it than Mf. Maginn, and the town had suffered a great loss. lie took a greater interest in the town, and their deepest sympathy would go out to his widow and family.

CONDOLENCE PROM UNITED IRISH LEAGUE A special and largely attended meeting of the War- rington Branch of the United Irish League was held ors I Monday evening. Mr. P. Delaney p residing. The chairman very feelingly referred to the great loss sustained by the Branch through the sad death of the President, Mr. J. Magian, J.P., end expressed sympathy with the widow and children. On the motion of Mr. r. Cunningham, seconded by Mr. C. Roddy, the following resolution was passed iv silence,the members standing :- 'That we, the members of the Warring ton Branch of the United Irish League deeply regret to learn of the sad death of our able and valued President, Mr. J. Maginn, J.P., and tender our heartfelt sympathy to his sorrowing wife and children." It was decided that a floral tribute taking the form of a harp, be procured.At the oonclusion of the meeting the members proceeded to the station and awaited the arrival of the train conveying the body. The coffin being placed in the hearse they formed two deep and led the mournfal procession to Raddon Court, leitchford, the residence of the deceased gentleman.

TESTIMONIAL PRESENTED.

Speech by Mr. J. A. Seddon, M.P.

DEATH OF MR. JOHN MAGINN, J.P.

THE STORY OF A STRENUOUS LIFE.

We announce with deep regret,which will be shared by all our readers, the death of Mr. John Maginn,J.P., of Raddon Court, who passed away at Leeds on Saturday evening. Mr. Magian, whose state of health had for a long time past given great anxiety to his relatives and friends, underwent a surgical operation at the Yorkshire Co-operative Nursing Homes, Hyde. terrace, Leeds, last Friday morning. The operation was entirely successful, but it was of a severe nature, and the shock consequent upon it was more than Mr. Maginn in his reduced state of bodily health could withstand. For some time after it took place, 20 to 21 hours, he seemed to be dcing well, and showed every prospect of recovering, but-at an early hour on Saturday morning he became worse and gradually sank The surgeons in charge, however, had hopes he would rally, and, to prevent all chance of the excitement acting adversely on him, would not allow his relatives to see hint until Saturday at noon. About that time it was evidenthe was losing strength fast, and his wife and brothers were allowed into the room. He was quite conscious and calm,and even then spoke hopefully, but it,was easy to see his distress was increasing every moment. During the afternoon he became restless, desiring his position to be constantly changed, and thence forward he sank rapidly. While the last sacraments of the Catholic Church were being administered by the Rev. Father O'Connor, of Leeds, he made the responses in a firm, clear voice, which showed no trace of fear of his impending death. Only a little while later, however, he lost consciousness momentarily, but recovering again he pressed his wife's hand, and uttering a few words quietly, breathed his last -

It was characteristic of the man that he made all the arrangements himself for this operation, and would not allow any relative to accmapany him or be neat' while it was taking place. He made light of it to his relatives in his peculiar, self-reliant way, but we learn that he was cognisant of the probability of its ending fatally, for on Monday week, when he officiated as a magistrate for the last time, he referred to the occasion as the final act of his magisterial office. He also, in conversation with other friends, made refer- ence to the possibility of never seeing them again. He leaves a widow and five young children, and a host of admiring friends to mourn his loss.

The body w—as brought from Leeda to Raddon Court on Monday evening, and was met at Bank Quay Station by a number of representatives of the different Irish and Catholic bodies in the town.

THE LATE 'At

LOCAL WEDDINGS.

Wallington— Atkinson.

There was a pretty wedding at Wycliffe Congrega- tional Church on Tnesday, when Alr, Tom F. Wallington, son of Mr. George Wallington, Froghall- lane, was married t 3 Miss Henrietta (Dolly) Atkinson, daughter of Mr. Henry Atkinon, Eaglescliffe, Latchford. Great interest was taken in the happy event and a large congregation assembled. The church was tastefully decorated with flowers and plants. The Rev. John Yong° officiated. Mr. W. 11. Payton, Mits. Bee., presided at the organ and played choice 3 elect"., including Mendelssohn's " Wedding March." The bride, who was given away by her father, was attended by Miss Minnie Pell, of Kirkby Lonsclale, and Miss Annie Wallington. Mr. George Wallington, jun., discharged the duties of b.st man. After the ceremony a reception was held in Wyclifie Memorial Buildings, a large number of guests attending. Later the happy couple left for the Lake District where the honeymoon is being spent. They were the recipients of many handsome presents.

Milne—Ewart.

THE POLICE SPORTS.

The Police Sports have become 'Rl:Tilly established among our local athletic meetings and the attendance cf 4,000 on the Wilderspaol enclosure on Thursday after- noon showed that the festival is not waning in popular- ity. Our local custodians of the peace are ever ready to respond to the call of charity and once more the proceeds were devoted to such worthy objects as the Warrington Infirmary .and other institutions and the Police Athletic club. From beginning to end the sports were most interesting. Unfortunately the fine weather did not hold out till the . close of the

meeting, for towards tea-time rain began to fan heavily and the attendance was speedilY thinned. The programme was admirably carried through and although there were several spills in the cycle races no one was seriously hurt. C. Berry,the local crack, had i the misfortune to come a cropper n the two miles and this spoiled his chances. T. Sherratt, the famous rider, won his heat in grand style from virtual scratch, but declined to turn out in the final, as he considered it risky with the track in such a slippery state. The entries for the sports constituted a record and included many noted athletes. Local competitors acquitted themselves fairly well, a fine victory being i that of Reuben Bennett, Warrington, n the one mile flat race. Praise is due to the • following officials for the excellence of the arrange- ments :—President, Mr MNicholls, Chief Constable ; vice-presidents, Detective Superintendent Monaghan, Inspectors Williamson, Smith, Foster, and Hod,gkiss ; judges, Alderman D. Tinnion, J.P, Messrs Harvey, 'l' Looker, J.P, Babbitt, D Wyllie F Barber, Pickavano and Booth ; N.C.O ; timekeeper:Mr, E Warren ; marks- men, Messrs F Crabtree, Birchall, W Carter, Reece, Whittaker, J Beardsall, P Coates, W Bellian and Astill ; stewards, Ex-Sergeant Evans, Sergeants Barlow, Stewart, Detective Meacock, P.O's Clough, Newsy, Hayes, Tolley' Wyse, Burrows and Rylate ; clerk of the course, Inspector Hodgkiss and P.O Hesp ; starter. Mr T Wainwright, Salford Harriers; secretary, P.O T Bennett ; assistant secretary, P.O Whitby. During tho afternoon the Blue Coat School Baud played selections and there was a military tournament by a contingent of soldiers from Orford Barracks,under the direction of Instructors Sergeant Major Scott and 'Sergeant Knight.

FINEST FLOWER SHOW. Many Titled Winn—e; at the Shrewsbury

Fete. Shrewsbury floral fke—the finest of its kind in the. country—was held on Wednesday and Thursday end attracted many thousands of visitors to the old town - The feature of the show was the wonderful display of roses, some of them being almost as big as cabbages. The sweet peas were also remarkably fine. Apart frora the beauty of the exhibits, there were many attractione for the thousands of visitors who flocked to Shrewsbury from all parts. Three regimental bands provided music, the Spencer family supervised balloon ascents,and there were horse-leaping competitions. In the great group class Messrs. Cypher, of Chelten- ham, again swept the board. Over £lOO was offered prizes for a collection of fruit arranged for decorative effect, and the premier prize was secured by the Duke' of Westminster, with the Earl of Harrington second, Mr. Granville Farquhar third, and the Duke of Portland fourth. For bridal bouquets Messrs. Felton and Sons, ef Hanover-square, and W. G. Garner, of Altrinchanit secured the chief awards. The principal prize for 3 collection of grapes was won by thc Earl of Harrington: and other prominent winners were Lord Belper, Loru" Harlech, Lady Ashburton, the Marquis of Northarnptea' Sir P. A. Muntz, M.P., Lord Trevor. Sir D. Duckwortlii King, the Earl of Lathom. Sir T Hesketh, Bart., [Pr Hatherton, and the Hon. Vicary Gibbs.

NEW WORKS IN ENGI AND. Results of the New Patents Act. Messrs. Leopold Farmer and Sons, surveyors and auctioneers, of Gresham-street, London, in a letter to the " Daily Chronicle," pointing out the beneficial results that have accrued through the new Patents Act, say : The foreigner affected by the Act bad to look to the future to protect his manufacture, with the result that the following important firms have acquired land and factories in England to carry on their business : 1. Elberfelder Farbenfabriken (represented by the Bayer Company, Limited) acquired 24 acres of land at Port Sunlight, Cheshire. 2. Iloschliefarwerke Company acquired seven acres of land at Ellesmere Port. Cheshire. 3. Gillette Razor Company, works, Leicester. 4. Sanatogen Food Company, land, Cornwall. -5. Pintsch Suction Gas Plant Company, works, London. 6. National Cash Register Company, works, London. "We feel certain, having gauged the feeling abroad of the effect the passing of the Patent and Designs Act is havirg on manufacturers, that the movement is only beginning, and there will he a big development of it, principally amongst German manufacturers'who are makers of numerous chemical products largely used in dyeing, etc. The American industry, being more in machinery, is not so much affected, but from the m:tch electrical, pottery, and other industries we may have lima opening up works in England."
